NS5-brane backgrounds and coset CFT partition functions
Andrea Dei and Emil J. Martinec

Abstract
Worldsheet string theory is solvable for a variety of backgrounds involving Neveu-Schwarz fivebranes, in terms of gauged nonlinear sigma models on group manifolds. We compute the worldsheet torus partition function of these models, and propose gauging of null isometries as a unifying principle and conceptual framework for this large family of string backgrounds. In the process, we explain how partition functions of asymmetrically gauged Wess-Zumino-Witten models can be computed from the path integral, and organize and systematize various results scattered throughout the literature.



1
Introduction

There are few curved backgrounds in string theory for which an exact worldsheet description is available. Those that are known involve Wess-Zumino-Witten (WZW) models, and the coset models that arise when they are gauged (GWZW models).
The WZW model is the non-linear sigma model on a group manifold
G
, with a covariantly constant Neveu-Schwarz 3-form flux
H_{3}
given by the group structure constants. The action is invariant under left and right group transformations of the sigma model field
g\mapsto h_{L}\,g\,h_{R}
. One can then gauge any non-anomalous subgroup
H\subset G_{L}\times G_{R}
. From these constructions (including flat spacetime and tori), a further set of models can be obtained via quotienting by discrete symmetries,
i.e.
the orbifold procedure
Dixon:1985jw
.

A particularly rich set of examples involves backgrounds sourced by
n_{5}
Neveu-Schwarz (NS) fivebranes:

•

The Euclidean black NS5-brane geometry in the decoupling limit
g_{s}\to 0
is the coset model
Witten:1991yr
;
Dijkgraaf:1991ba

\frac{SL(2,\mathds{R})_{n_{5}}}{U(1)}\times{SU(2)}_{n_{5}}\times ds^{2}_{\parallel}\ ,

(1.1)

where
ds^{2}_{\parallel}
is the spatial geometry along the fivebranes.

•

The geometry of
{n_{5}}
separated BPS fivebranes is described in the same decoupling limit by the coset orbifold
Giveon:1999px
;
Giveon:1999tq

\bigg(\frac{SL(2,\mathds{R})_{n_{5}}}{U(1)}\times\frac{{SU(2)}_{n_{5}}}{U(1)}\bigg)\Big/{\mathbb{Z}}_{n_{5}}\times ds^{2}_{\parallel}\ ,

(1.2)

•

The decoupling limit of
{n_{5}}
NS5-branes and
{n_{1}}
fundamental strings is the WZW model

SL(2,\mathds{R})_{n_{5}}\times{SU(2)}_{n_{5}}\times\mathcal{M}\ ,

(1.3)

where
\mathcal{M}={\mathbb{T}}^{4}
or
K3

Giveon:1998ns
;
Kutasov:1999xu
;
Maldacena:2000hw
.

•

The Euclidean BTZ black hole is the orbifold
Banados:1992wn
;
Banados:1992gq
;
Natsuume:1996ij

\bigg(\frac{SL(2,{\mathbb{C}})_{n_{5}}}{{SU(2)}_{n_{5}}}\bigg)\Big/{\mathbb{Z}}\times{SU(2)}_{n_{5}}\times\mathcal{M}\ .

(1.4)

Recently, a large class of 1/2-BPS “supertube” geometries
Lunin:2001fv
have been shown to be described by the cosets
Martinec:2017ztd
;
Martinec:2018nco
;
Martinec:2019wzw
;
Martinec:2020gkv
;
Bufalini:2021ndn
;
Bufalini:2022wyp
;
Bufalini:2022wzu
;
Martinec:2022okx

\frac{SL(2,\mathds{R})_{n_{5}}\times{SU(2)}_{n_{5}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{t}\times U(1)_{y}}{U(1)_{L}\times U(1)_{R}}~,

(1.5)

where
t
(
y
) parametrize timelike (spacelike) factors in the target space.
These are highly coherent states far from the vacuum, describing fivebranes carrying momentum and/or string winding charge, as well as
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
angular momentum, which nevertheless admit an exact description in terms of worldsheet string theory. This construction extends to a larger class of backgrounds by varying the embedding of the gauge group to involve asymmetrically gauged cosets, where
U(1)_{L}
and
U(1)_{R}
are distinct subgroups of
G
. Examples include the 1/4-BPS “GLMT”
Giusto:2004id
;
Giusto:2012yz
and non-supersymmetric “JMaRT”
Jejjala:2005yu
;
Chakrabarty:2015foa
geometries obtained by spacetime spectral flow of the 1/2-BPS backgrounds.
In the
AdS_{3}
limit
R_{y}\to\infty
, there is also a worldsheet construction of the backgrounds (
1.5
) as global orbifolds
(SL(2,\mathds{R})\times{SU(2)})/{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathsf{k}}

Martinec:2001cf
;
Martinec:2023zha
.

Holographically, the construction (
1.5
) realizes particular heavy states having conformal dimension of order
N={n_{1}}{n_{5}}
in the dual spacetime CFT of central charge
c=6N

Lunin:2001jy
;
Giusto:2012yz
;
Chakrabarty:2015foa
.
1
1

1

The coset models (
1.5
) build states in the Ramond sector of the spacetime CFT, while the
(AdS_{3}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{3})/{\mathbb{Z}}_{\mathsf{k}}
orbifold construction builds the corresponding states in the NS sector.
Perturbative fluctuations around these particular heavy states, including stringy excitations, are captured by the worldsheet theory.
The construction (
1.2
) has been used to calculate various properties of little string theory, for instance the perturbative string S-matrix
Aharony:2004xn
; also, the elliptic genus
Eguchi:2004yi
;
Ashok:2012qy
;
Giveon:2015raa
, which describes a large class of 1/4-BPS states.
The worldsheet partition function on the Euclidean BTZ background (
1.4
) has been computed in
Maldacena:2000kv
;
Ashok:2020dnc
;
Ashok:2022vdz
, and related to the spectrum of (stringy) quasinormal modes.

This wide variety of examples of exactly solvable string backgrounds with NS flux motivates a study of the worldsheet partition function of these models, as the generating function of the perturbative string spectrum. Most of the above examples involve gauging of the current algebra symmetries of the worldsheet sigma-model. The most elaborate of these gauged WZW models, eq. (
1.5
), involve the gauging of null isometries of the background; but we will see that
all
of the above coset models can be recast as null-gauged WZW models. In this way, null gauging serves as a sort of unifying principle.

Computing the worldsheet torus partition function of the GWZW models (
1.5
) presents two challenges: The group
G
is non-compact, and in general asymmetrically gauged. Since
G
is not compact, the associated partition function cannot be computed simply by following standard CFT
2
methods, such as tracing over the Hilbert space (see
DiFrancesco:1997nk
for a review). The only known approach is the path integral
Gawedzki:1991yu
, where the subtleties of the correct measure for integrating over a continuum of group representations can be sorted out. In fact, partition functions for non-compact GWZW models have been computed for all the examples (
1.1
)-(
1.4
),
Gawedzki:1991yu
;
Kutasov:2000jp
;
Maldacena:2000kv
;
Hanany:2002ev
;
Israel:2003ry
;
Israel:2004ir
;
Ashok:2020dnc
. While in all these cases the group
G
was non-compact, the gauging was symmetric,
i.e.
it did not involve a different treatment of holomorphic and anti-holomorphic currents. The consideration of non-simple groups opens up a much larger choice of embeddings of the gauge group
Martinec:2017ztd
;
Bufalini:2021ndn
, including asymmetric embeddings.
In this manuscript we deal with both these issues, and extend previous treatments in the literature, in which only one of these ingredients is present.
2
2

2

A notable exception is
DAppollonio:2007ldj
;
DAppollonio:2008xxq
, which analyzes asymmetric cosets of
H_{4}
, the plane-wave limit of
SL(2,\mathds{R})\times U(1)
, and computes the partition function.

We mentioned that the isometries gauged in the models (
1.5
) are
null
. In order to convey the intuition behind many of the computations we carry out in this manuscript, let us briefly sketch the origin of some special feautures arising when the gauged isometries are null. When gauging
H\subset G_{L}\times G_{R}
, usually one of the two components of the
\mathfrak{h}
-valued gauge field
A_{\mu}
is locally pure gauge, while the other is a physical degree of freedom. One can then locally rewrite the physical component of the gauge field in terms of
H
-valued fields
h
, together with the zero modes of
A_{\mu}
.
One then employs the Polyakov-Wiegmann identity
Polyakov:1984et

\kappa S^{\text{WZW}}(g)-\frac{\kappa}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}z\,\text{Tr}(g^{-1}\partial_{\bar{z}}g\,\partial_{z}hh^{-1})=\kappa S^{\text{WZW}}(gh)-\kappa S^{\text{WZW}}(h)~,

(1.6)

to decouple
A_{\mu}
(parametrized in terms of
h
) from the path integral over
g\in G
.
Schematically, the path integral over
G
with the subgroup
H\subset G_{L}\times G_{R}
gauged yields a generalization of the LHS involving both chiralities, which after a systematic use of the Polyakov-Wiegmann identity yields a corresponding generalization of the RHS. One then absorbs the appearance of
h
in the first term via a field redefinition to arrive at a decoupled path integral over
G\times H
.

On the other hand, when the action of
H
on
G
is such that the gauge orbits are null isometries (as is the case for the models (
1.5
) of
Martinec:2017ztd
, see also
Klimcik:1994wp
;
Israel:2004ir
),
both
components of
A_{\mu}
are pure gauge – there is a
second
gauge invariance under a group
\widetilde{H}
with a closely related embedding into
G_{L}\times G_{R}
, and the single gauge field
A_{\mu}
is sufficient to gauge both
H
and
\widetilde{H}
.
In this situation,
both
components of
A_{\mu}
are pure gauge, and can be eliminated apart from zero modes by a choice of gauge. One is then left with an integral over
G
, together with the zero modes of
A_{\mu}
.

This fact suggests that ordinary GWZW models can be recast as null-gauged models, simply by gauging
G\times H
instead of
G
, with a “wrong-sign” kinetic term for
H
(as on the RHS of (
1.6
) above). With the diagonal embedding of
H
into
G_{L}\times H_{L}
and into
G_{R}\times H_{R}
, the gauge orbits are null isometries. Using the gauge symmetry to eliminate the gauge field apart from zero modes, one is left with a path integral over
G\times H
, together with the gauge field zero modes, and thus formally the standard GWZW construction is equivalent to null gauging of
G\times H
.
However, the manipulations of the path integral involved are rather delicate; in particular, we have been rather cavalier in the treatment of the gauge field zero modes in the above discussion.

After reviewing the WZW model and its gauging at the start of Section
2
, we go on to show how null gauging renders all but the Wilson line degrees of freedom of the gauge field to be gauge-redundant. As warmup exercises in the computation of more general null-gauged WZW partition functions, we show in Sections
3
and
4
how to recover the standard
\frac{{SU(2)}}{U(1)}
and
\frac{SL(2,\mathds{R})}{U(1)}
GWZW partition functions from null gauging of
{SU(2)}\times\mathds{R}
and
SL(2,\mathds{R})\times U(1)
, respectively.

Null-gauging can of course be applied to more general examples than those that arise from the conventional coset construction.
A more nontrivial example of null gauging arises in the example of
G=SL(2,\mathds{R})\times{SU(2)}
, which we turn to in Section
5
. We show there how it reproduces the partition function of the model (
1.2
) of
Giveon:1999px
, where a background of NS5-branes on their Coulomb branch is described by the coset orbifold
\big(\frac{SL(2,\mathds{R})}{U(1)}\times\frac{{SU(2)}}{U(1)}\big)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{n_{5}}
.
In this case, the gauge group
H
is not a factor in
G
, and the equivalence to the standard coset construction is less straightforward (as the additional orbifold in the relation between the two suggests).

Finally, we come in Section
6
to the models of
Martinec:2017ztd
describing NS5-F1 “supertubes” and related backgrounds. Here the group
G
is
SL(2,\mathds{R})\times{SU(2)}\times\mathds{R}\times U(1)
, see eq. (
1.5
), with
H=U(1)
embedded non-trivially across all the factors in
G
. The null gauging procedure is at present the only known realization of these models, and the construction of their partition functions is our main result. See in particular eq. (
6.2.3
). The decomposition of this result into characters (see eqs. (
6.69
) and (
6.88
)) gives us confidence in the result, and confirms the analysis of the spectrum carried out in
e.g.

Martinec:2018nco
;
Martinec:2022okx
.

We conclude with a discussion in Section
7
, where we list a number of possible applications of our results. Several appendices address some technical points and provide details of the various component theta series, affine characters,
etc
. In particular, Appendix
E
provides a detailed treatment of the gauging of fermions in the supersymmetric asymmetric GWZW model, which to our knowledge has not appeared previously in the literature.

2
Review of Gauged WZW models

In view of the discussion on more general cosets in the next section, let us review the best known examples of GWZW: vector and axial gauging. We will refer to these two cosets as “symmetric” GWZW, as opposed to “asymmetric” gauging, for which the currents to be gauged on the left and the right are different.

The WZW action for a simple group
G
reads
Witten:1983ar
;
DiFrancesco:1997nk
3
3

3

For a semi-simple group, the action is simply given by the sum of terms of the form (
2.1
) for each simple factor.

\displaystyle\kappa S^{\text{WZW}}(g)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa}{8\pi}\int_{\Sigma}\text{d}^{2}x\,\text{Tr}(g^{-1}\partial^{\mu}gg^{-1}\partial_{\mu}g)-\frac{i\kappa}{12\pi}\int_{B}\text{d}^{3}x\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}\,\text{Tr}(g^{-1}\partial_{\mu}gg^{-1}\partial_{\nu}gg^{-1}\partial_{\rho}g)\ ,

(2.1)

where
g\in G
,
\kappa\in\mathds{N}
and
B
is a three-dimensional manifold having as boundary the compactification of the base space
\Sigma
. It is easy to show that the action (
2.1
) obeys the Polyakov-Wiegmann identity written above,

\kappa S^{\text{WZW}}(gh)=\kappa S^{\text{WZW}}(g)+\kappa S^{\text{WZW}}(h)-\frac{\kappa}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\text{Tr}(g^{-1}\partial_{\bar{v}}g\,\partial_{v}hh^{-1})\ ,

(2.2)

where we introduced holomorphic coordinates
4
4

4

For the integral measure we adopt the following conventions,
\text{d}^{2}v=\text{d Re}(v)\,\text{d Im}(v)=\text{d}x^{0}\text{d}x^{1}=\text{d}^{2}x
.

v=x^{0}+ix^{1}\ ,\qquad\bar{v}=x^{0}-ix^{1}\ .

(2.3)

The Polyakov-Wiegmann identity makes manifest the gauge invariance of the action (
2.1
) under
5
5

5

Different conventions are frequently adopted in the math literature, where the gauge invariance is often written as
g\to h_{R}gh_{L}
. We prefer to adopt the conventions of
Quella:2002fk
.

g(v,\bar{v})\to h_{L}(v)g(v,\bar{v})h_{R}(\bar{v})\ ,

(2.4)

where
h_{L}(v)
and
h_{R}(\bar{v})
are arbitrary (anti-) holomorphic functions taking values in a subgroup
H\subset G
. The coset theory
G/H
can be obtained by gauging the global symmetry (
2.4
) and promoting it to

g(v,\bar{v})\to h_{L}(v,\bar{v})g(v,\bar{v})h_{R}(v,\bar{v})\ ,

(2.5)

where
h_{L}
and
h_{R}
are again valued in some subgroup
H
of
G
but are no longer (anti-)holomorphic. The best known non-anomalous gauging for a simple group
G
and a semi-simple subgroup
H
is

g(v,\bar{v})\to h(v,\bar{v})g(v,\bar{v})h^{-1}(v,\bar{v})\ ,

(2.6)

known as vector gauging. When instead
H
contains
U(1)
factors, for each
U(1)
factor, in addition to vector gauging also axial gauging is non-anomalous. The latter corresponds to

g(v,\bar{v})\to h^{-1}(v,\bar{v})g(v,\bar{v})h^{-1}(v,\bar{v})\ .

(2.7)

The vector gauged action
Gawedzki:1988hq
;
Karabali:1988au
;
Park:1989ae
;
Karabali:1989dk
;
Bardakci:1990lbc
;
Kiritsis:1991zt

\kappa S^{V}(g,\mathcal{A})=\kappa S^{\text{WZW}}(g)+\frac{\kappa}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\text{Tr}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}\partial_{v}gg^{-1}-\mathcal{A}_{v}g^{-1}\partial_{\bar{v}}g+g^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}g\mathcal{A}_{v}-\mathcal{A}_{v}\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}\right)

(2.8)

is gauge-invariant under

g\mapsto hgh^{-1}\ ,\qquad\mathcal{A}_{v}\mapsto h\mathcal{A}_{v}h^{-1}+\partial_{v}h\,h^{-1}\ ,\qquad\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}\mapsto h\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}h^{-1}+\partial_{\bar{v}}h\,h^{-1}\ ,

(2.9)

while for the axial gauged action

\kappa S^{A}(g,\mathcal{A})=\kappa S^{\text{WZW}}(g)-\frac{\kappa}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\text{Tr}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}\partial_{v}gg^{-1}+\mathcal{A}_{v}g^{-1}\partial_{\bar{v}}g+g^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}g\mathcal{A}_{v}+\mathcal{A}_{v}\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}\right)\ ,

(2.10)

gauge transformations read

g\mapsto h^{-1}gh^{-1}\ ,\qquad\mathcal{A}_{v}\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{v}+\partial_{v}h\,h^{-1}\ ,\qquad\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}+\partial_{\bar{v}}h\,h^{-1}\ .

(2.11)

The WZW action (
2.1
) can be promoted to an
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetric action
Abdalla:1984ef
;
DiVecchia:1984nyg
;
Figueroa-OFarrill:1996xek

S^{\text{WZW}}_{\mathcal{N}=1}(g)=(\kappa-h^{\vee})S^{\text{WZW}}(g)+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\text{Tr}\left(\psi\partial_{\bar{v}}\psi+\tilde{\psi}\partial_{v}\tilde{\psi}\right)\ ,

(2.12)

where
h^{\vee}
is the dual Coxeter number of
\mathfrak{g}
,
i.e.
the quadratic Casimir of the adjoint representation divided by 2 and the free fermions
\psi
and
\tilde{\psi}
take values in the adjoint representation of
\mathfrak{g}
. The supersymmetric vector gauged action reads
Schnitzer:1988qj
;
Witten:1991mk
;
Nakatsu:1991pu
;
Tseytlin:1993my
;
Figueroa-OFarrill:1996xek

S^{V}_{\mathcal{N}=1}(g,\mathcal{A})=(\kappa-h^{\vee})S^{V}(g,\mathcal{A})+\int\frac{\text{d}^{2}v}{2\pi}\,\text{Tr}\left(\psi\partial_{\bar{v}}\psi-\psi\,[\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}},\psi]+\tilde{\psi}\partial_{v}\tilde{\psi}-\tilde{\psi}[\mathcal{A}_{v},\tilde{\psi}]\right)\ ,

(2.13)

while for axial gauging we have

S^{A}_{\mathcal{N}=1}(g,\mathcal{A})=(\kappa-h^{\vee})S^{A}(g,\mathcal{A})+\int\frac{\text{d}^{2}v}{2\pi}\,\text{Tr}\left(\psi\partial_{\bar{v}}\psi+\psi\,[\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}},\psi]+\tilde{\psi}\partial_{v}\tilde{\psi}-\tilde{\psi}[\mathcal{A}_{v},\tilde{\psi}]\right)\ ,

(2.14)

where the worldsheet fermions
\psi
and
\tilde{\psi}
take value in
\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}
, the orthogonal complement of
\mathfrak{h}
.

2.1
Asymmetric gauging

A richer class of gauged WZW models arises when gauging different subgroups
H_{L}
on the left and
H_{R}
on the right of a (not necessarily simple) group
G
by promoting eq. (
2.5
) to

g\to\epsilon_{L}(h)\,g\,\epsilon_{R}(h^{-1})\ ,

(2.15)

with
\epsilon_{L}
and
\epsilon_{R}
(a priori different) Lie group homomorphisms from
H
to
G
, satisfying

\epsilon_{L}(g_{1}\,g_{2})=\epsilon_{L}(g_{1})\,\epsilon_{L}(g_{2})\ ,\qquad\epsilon_{R}(g_{1}\,g_{2})=\epsilon_{R}(g_{1})\,\epsilon_{R}(g_{2})\ ,\qquad\forall\,g_{1},g_{2}\in G\ .

(2.16)

With some slight abuse of notation, we will denote by
\epsilon_{L}
and
\epsilon_{R}
also the corresponding embeddings of the Lie algebras, which are Lie algebra homomorphisms satisfying

\displaystyle\epsilon_{L}([B,C])

\displaystyle=[\epsilon_{L}(B),\epsilon_{L}(C)]\ ,\qquad\forall\,B,C\in\mathfrak{h}_{L}\ ,

(2.17)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{R}([B,C])

\displaystyle=[\epsilon_{R}(B),\epsilon_{R}(C)]\ ,\qquad\forall\,B,C\in\mathfrak{h}_{R}\ .

Let us assume that both
G
and
H
are reductive,
i.e.
they can be decomposed into a product of simple and
U(1)
factors. When gauging a subgroup
H=H_{1}\times\dots\times H_{r}
of a group
G=G_{1}\times\dots\times G_{n}
, the asymmetric gauged action takes the form
Guadagnini:1987ty
;
Guadagnini:1987qc
;
Witten:1991mm
;
Bars:1991pt
;
Quella:2002fk

S^{B}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{i}^{B}S^{B}_{i}\ ,

(2.18)

where the superscript
B
denotes the bosonic part of the action

S^{B}_{i}=S^{\text{WZW}}(g_{i})+\sum_{s=1}^{r}\int\frac{\text{d}^{2}v}{\pi}\text{Tr}_{i}\Bigl[\epsilon_{L}^{i,s}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}^{s})\,\partial_{v}g_{i}g_{i}^{-1}-\epsilon_{R}^{i,s}(\mathcal{A}_{v}^{s})\,g_{i}^{-1}\partial_{\bar{v}}g_{i}+\epsilon_{L}^{i,s}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}^{s})\,g_{i}\,\epsilon_{R}^{i,s}(\mathcal{A}_{v}^{s})\,g_{i}^{-1}\\
-\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon_{L}^{i,s}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}^{s})\,\epsilon_{L}^{i,s}(\mathcal{A}_{v}^{s})-\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon_{R}^{i,s}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}^{s})\,\epsilon_{R}^{i,s}(\mathcal{A}_{v}^{s})\Bigr]

(2.19)

and we split the embeddings
\epsilon_{L}
and
\epsilon_{R}
respectively into

\epsilon_{L}^{i,s}:H_{s}\hookrightarrow G_{i}\ ,\qquad\epsilon_{R}^{i,s}:H_{s}\hookrightarrow G_{i}\ ,\qquad i=1,\dots,n\ ,\qquad s=1,\dots,r\ .

(2.20)

In eq. (
2.18
),
\kappa_{i}^{B}
denotes the bosonic level of the
i
-th factor in the decomposition
G=G_{1}\times\dots\times G_{n}
. For the gauging to be non-anomalous one should require that
Bars:1991pt
;
Witten:1991mm
;
Figueroa-OFarrill:1994vwl
;
Quella:2002fk

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{i}\,\text{Tr}_{i}\{\epsilon_{L}^{s,i}(B)\,\epsilon_{L}^{s,i}(C)\}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{i}\,\text{Tr}_{i}\{\epsilon_{R}^{s,i}(B)\,\epsilon_{R}^{s,i}(C)\}\ ,\qquad\forall\,B,C\in\mathfrak{h}_{s}\ .

(2.21)

When considering bosonic GWZW models,
\kappa_{i}
in (
2.21
) reads
\kappa_{i}=\kappa^{B}_{i}
, which is the level entering the bosonic action (
2.18
). We will introduce in a moment
\mathcal{N}=1
asymmetric GWZW and see that in this case,
\kappa_{i}=\kappa^{B}_{i}+h^{\vee}_{i}
, where
h^{\vee}_{i}
is the dual Coxeter number of
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
.
6
6

6

More precisely,
\kappa_{i}=\kappa_{i}^{B}+h^{\vee}_{i}
for each factor of
\mathfrak{g}
containing charged fermions. This distinction will not play a role for us.

Using eq. (
2.21
) one can show that the action (
2.18
) is invariant under the infinitesimal gauge transformation

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{A}^{s}_{v}=i\partial_{v}\omega^{s}+i[\omega^{s},\mathcal{A}^{s}_{v}]\ ,\quad\delta\mathcal{A}^{s}_{\bar{v}}=i\partial_{\bar{v}}\omega^{s}+i[\omega^{s},\mathcal{A}^{s}_{\bar{v}}]\ ,\quad\delta g_{i}=i\epsilon_{L}(\omega_{s})\,g_{i}-ig_{i}\,\epsilon_{R}(\omega_{s})\ .

(2.22)

In fact, the variation of the action reads
Quella:2002fk

\delta S^{B}=i\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{s=1}^{r}\frac{\kappa_{i}^{B}}{2\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\text{Tr}_{i}\Bigl[\epsilon_{L}^{i,s}(\mathcal{A}^{s}_{\bar{v}})\partial_{v}\epsilon_{L}^{i,s}(\omega_{s})-\epsilon_{R}^{i,s}(\mathcal{A}^{s}_{\bar{v}})\partial_{v}\epsilon_{R}^{i,s}(\omega_{s})\\
-\epsilon_{L}^{i,s}(\mathcal{A}^{s}_{v})\partial_{\bar{v}}\epsilon_{L}^{i,s}(\omega_{s})+\epsilon_{R}^{i,s}(\mathcal{A}^{s}_{v})\partial_{\bar{v}}\epsilon_{R}^{i,s}(\omega_{s})\Bigr]\ ,

(2.23)

which vanishes upon using eq. (
2.21
) with
\kappa_{i}=\kappa_{i}^{B}
. Notice that the vector and axial gauged actions (
2.8
) and (
2.10
) can be recovered from eq. (
2.19
) by dropping the index
i
and setting

\epsilon^{\rm vec}_{L}(\mathcal{A}_{v})=\epsilon^{\rm vec}_{R}(\mathcal{A}_{v})=\mathcal{A}_{v}~~,\qquad\epsilon^{\rm vec}_{L}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}})=\epsilon^{\rm vec}_{R}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}})=\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}\ ,

(2.24)

and

\epsilon^{\rm ax}_{L}(\mathcal{A}_{v})=-\epsilon^{\rm ax}_{R}(\mathcal{A}_{v})=-\mathcal{A}_{v}~~,\qquad\epsilon^{\rm ax}_{L}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}})=-\epsilon^{\rm ax}_{R}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}})=-\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}\ ,

(2.25)

respectively.

In the following we will mainly consider gauged WZW models with
H=U(1)
and hence
r=1
. We will thus often drop the
s
label. Moreover, we will be interested in supersymmetric GWZW model, for which the asymmetrically gauged action (
2.18
) will be accompanied by a corresponding asymmetrically gauged action of left- and right-moving fermions transforming in the adjoint of
\mathfrak{g}
. The action of supersymmetric GWZW takes the schematic form

S=S^{B}+S^{F}\ ,\qquad\text{with}\qquad S^{B}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{i}^{B}S_{i}^{B}\ ,

(2.26)

and

S^{F}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\text{Tr}\Bigl(\psi\,\partial_{\bar{v}}\psi-\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum_{s}$}}\psi\,[\epsilon_{L}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}^{s}),\psi]+\tilde{\psi}\,\partial_{v}\tilde{\psi}-\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum_{s}$}}\tilde{\psi}\,[\epsilon_{R}(\mathcal{A}_{v}^{s}),\tilde{\psi}]\Bigr)\ ,

(2.27)

where we denoted by
\psi
and
\tilde{\psi}
respectively holomorphic and anti-holomorphic free fermions, taking values in the Lie algebra of
G
. As we review in Appendix
E
, the fermions in
\mathfrak{h}_{L}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{R}
decouple, resulting in an action of the same form, but with the fermions taking value respectively in
\mathfrak{h}_{L}^{\perp}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{R}^{\perp}
. One can easily check that the fermionic action (
2.27
) is classically invariant under the gauge transformations

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{A}_{v}^{s}

\displaystyle=i\partial_{v}\omega^{s}+i[\omega^{s},\mathcal{A}_{v}^{s}]\ ,

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}^{s}

\displaystyle=i\partial_{\bar{v}}\omega^{s}+i[\omega^{s},\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}^{s}]\ ,

(2.28)

\displaystyle\delta\psi

\displaystyle=i[\epsilon_{L}(\omega^{s}),\psi]\ ,

\displaystyle\delta\tilde{\psi}

\displaystyle=i[\epsilon_{R}(\omega^{s}),\tilde{\psi}]\ .

While the action (
2.27
) is gauge invariant, the path integral measure is not,

\prod_{A}\prod_{\tilde{A}}\mathcal{D}\psi_{A}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\psi}_{\tilde{A}}\to\prod_{A}\prod_{\tilde{A}}\mathcal{D}\psi_{A}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\psi}_{\tilde{A}}\,e^{\mathfrak{A}}\ ,

(2.29)

where we decomposed
\psi
and
\tilde{\psi}
over a basis of generators of
\mathfrak{h}_{L}^{\perp}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{R}^{\perp}
respectively and
A,\tilde{A}
run over
A\in\{1,\dots,\text{dim Adj}(\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}_{L})\}
and
\tilde{A}\in\{1,\dots,\text{dim Adj}(\mathfrak{h}^{\perp}_{R})\}
.
In all the examples we are going to encounter, the anomaly
\mathfrak{A}
in (
2.29
) cancels against the variation (
2.23
) of the bosonic action,

-\delta S^{B}+\mathfrak{A}=0\ ,

(2.30)

provided eq. (
2.21
) is obeyed with
\kappa_{i}=\kappa_{i}^{B}+h^{\vee}_{i}
, see also
Tseytlin:1992ri
;
Johnson:1994kv
.

2.2
Null gauging

Let us now consider a special instance of asymmetric GWZW models: null GWZW models
Klimcik:1994wp
;
Israel:2004ir
;
Martinec:2017ztd
. The gauging is said to be null when each side of eq. (
2.21
) independently vanishes,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{i}\,\text{Tr}_{i}\{\epsilon_{L}^{s,i}(B)\,\epsilon_{L}^{s,i}(C)\}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{i}\,\text{Tr}_{i}\{\epsilon_{R}^{s,i}(B)\,\epsilon_{R}^{s,i}(C)\}=0\ ,\qquad\forall\,B,C\in\mathfrak{h}_{s}\ .

(2.31)

In this case, the action (
2.26
) is invariant under the additional symmetry

\displaystyle\delta g_{i}

\displaystyle=-i\epsilon_{L}(\check{\omega}_{s})\,g_{i}-ig_{i}\,\epsilon_{R}(\check{\omega}_{s})\ ,

(2.32)

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{A}^{s}_{v}

\displaystyle=i\partial_{v}\check{\omega}_{s}+i[\check{\omega}_{s},\mathcal{A}^{s}_{v}]\ ,

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{A}^{s}_{\bar{v}}

\displaystyle=-i\partial_{\bar{v}}\check{\omega}_{s}-i[\check{\omega}_{s},\mathcal{A}^{s}_{\bar{v}}]\ ,

\displaystyle\delta\psi

\displaystyle=-i[\epsilon_{L}(\check{\omega}_{s}),\psi]\ ,

\displaystyle\delta\tilde{\psi}

\displaystyle=i[\epsilon_{R}(\check{\omega}_{s}),\tilde{\psi}]\ ,

which slightly differs from the gauge transformations (
2.22
) and (
2.28
) by reversing the direction of the null gauge orbits on the right relative to the null gauge orbits on the left. Similarly to the gauge invariance we discussed in the previous section, the variation of the bosonic action under (
2.32
) reads

\check{\delta}S^{B}=-i\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{s=1}^{r}\frac{\kappa_{i}^{B}}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\text{Tr}_{i}\Bigl[\epsilon_{L}^{i,s}(\mathcal{A}^{s}_{\bar{v}})\partial_{v}\epsilon_{L}^{i,s}(\check{\omega}_{s})+\epsilon_{R}^{i,s}(\mathcal{A}^{s}_{\bar{v}})\partial_{v}\epsilon_{R}^{i,s}(\check{\omega}_{s})\\
-\partial_{\bar{v}}\epsilon_{L}^{i,s}(\check{\omega}_{s})\epsilon_{L}^{i,s}(\mathcal{A}^{s}_{v})-\epsilon_{R}^{i,s}(\mathcal{A}^{s}_{v})\partial_{\bar{v}}\epsilon_{R}^{i,s}(\check{\omega}_{s})\Bigr]\ ,

(2.33)

while the fermionic action gives rise to the anomaly factor

\prod_{A}\prod_{\tilde{A}}\mathcal{D}\psi_{A}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\psi}_{\tilde{A}}\to\prod_{A}\prod_{\tilde{A}}\mathcal{D}\psi_{A}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\psi}_{\tilde{A}}\,e^{\check{\mathfrak{A}}}\ .

(2.34)

In all the examples of null gauging we consider, the anomaly (
2.34
) cancels against the variation (
2.33
),

-\check{\delta}S^{B}+\check{\mathfrak{A}}=0\ ,

(2.35)

provided eq. (
2.31
) is obeyed with
\kappa_{i}=\kappa_{i}^{B}+h^{\vee}_{i}
.

When the gauging is null, the fermions entering the action (
2.27
) take values in the “transverse space”
{\mathfrak{g}}_{L}^{{\sf t}}
(respectively
{\mathfrak{g}}_{R}^{{\sf t}}
), which is a subspace of
\mathfrak{h}_{L}^{\perp}
(respectively
\mathfrak{h}_{R}^{\perp}
), see eqs. (
E.69
) and (
E.70
). This is simply a consequence of the fact that when the gauging is null,
\mathfrak{h}_{L}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{L}^{\perp}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{R}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{R}^{\perp}
.
We show in Appendix
E
that one can also remove the fermions along
{\mathfrak{h}}_{L}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}_{R}
by a fermionic gauge transformation.
It follows that the labels
A
and
\tilde{A}
in eq. (
2.34
) run over the remaining directions
A\in\{1,\dots,\text{dim Adj}({\mathfrak{g}}_{L}^{{\sf t}})\}
and
\tilde{A}\in\{1,\dots,\text{dim Adj}({\mathfrak{g}}_{R}^{{\sf t}})\}
. We refer to Appendix
E
for a detailed derivation of this statement and for a discussion on its relation with the usual BRST decoupling of fermions in terms of
\beta\gamma
superghosts.

2.3
Parametrization of the gauge field

In the following sections we are going to compute partition functions of coset CFT’s by evaluating the path integral of the GWZW model. Let us review how gauge fields are usually parameterized in the literature
Karabali:1988au
;
Gawedzki:1988hq
;
Gawedzki:1988nj
;
Karabali:1989dk
;
Gawedzki:1991yu
;
Hanany:2002ev
;
Eguchi:2010cb
and spell out our conventions. We start by defining the worldsheet torus
\Sigma
with modular parameter
\tau
by

v=\sigma^{1}+\tau\sigma^{2}\ ,\qquad\bar{v}=\sigma^{1}+\bar{\tau}\sigma^{2}\ ,

(2.36)

where
\sigma^{1}
and
\sigma^{2}
parameterize
\alpha
and
\beta
cycles and range over

\sigma^{1}\in[0,2\pi)\ ,\qquad\sigma^{2}\in[0,2\pi)\ .

(2.37)

The embeddings
\epsilon_{L}^{i}(\mathcal{A}_{v})\in\mathfrak{h}_{L}
and
\epsilon_{R}^{i}(\mathcal{A}_{v})\in\mathfrak{h}_{R}
can be decomposed in terms of the generators
T^{i}_{L,a}\,,T^{i}_{R,a}
as

\displaystyle\epsilon_{L}^{i}(\mathcal{A}_{v})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a}T^{i}_{L,a}\,A^{a}_{v}~~,

\displaystyle\epsilon_{R}^{i}(\mathcal{A}_{v})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a}T^{i}_{R,a}\,A^{a}_{v}\ ,

(2.38)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{L}^{i}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a}T^{i}_{L,a}\,A^{a}_{\bar{v}}~~,

\displaystyle\epsilon_{R}^{i}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a}T^{i}_{R,a}\,A^{a}_{\bar{v}}\ ,

where
A^{a}_{v}
and
A^{a}_{\bar{v}}
are fields in the worldsheet coordinates
v,\bar{v}
. Since our interest here is
H=U(1)
, in the following we will simplify the notation and write the embedding in terms of a single field
A_{v}
and constant matrices
\varepsilon_{L}^{i}
,
\varepsilon_{R}^{i}
as

\epsilon_{L}^{i}(\mathcal{A}_{v})=\varepsilon_{L}^{i}A_{v}\ ,\qquad\epsilon_{R}^{i}(\mathcal{A}_{v})=\varepsilon_{R}^{i}A_{v}\ ,\qquad\epsilon_{L}^{i}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}})=\varepsilon_{L}^{i}A_{\bar{v}}\ ,\qquad\epsilon_{R}^{i}(\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}})=\varepsilon_{R}^{i}A_{\bar{v}}\ .

(2.39)

Using the Helmholtz-Hodge decomposition, the real gauge fields
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
can be parameterized as

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=\partial_{0}X-\partial_{1}Y-\frac{u_{1}}{\tau_{2}}\ ,

(2.40)

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=\partial_{1}X+\partial_{0}Y-\frac{u_{2}}{\tau_{2}}\ ,

where
X
is a real non-compact boson, and
Y
is a real compact boson with unit radius,

Y(\sigma_{1}+2\pi,\sigma_{2})=-2\pi m_{1}\ ,\qquad Y(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}+2\pi)=2\pi m_{2}\ ,\qquad\text{with}\qquad m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathds{Z}\ .

(2.41)

The Jacobian in the functional measure for the change of variables (
2.40
) leads as usual to a functional determinant that is represented by a set of Faddeev-Popov ghosts
b,c
and
\tilde{b},\tilde{c}
.

In the following, it will prove useful to introduce the notation

u=u_{1}+iu_{2}=s_{1}\tau+s_{2}\ ,

(2.42)

in terms of which the holonomies of the gauge field read

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\text{d}\sigma^{1}\,A_{\sigma^{1}}=-\frac{2\pi\,\tau_{1}}{\tau_{2}}(m_{1}+s_{1})\ ,\qquad\quad\int_{0}^{2\pi}\text{d}\sigma^{2}\,A_{\sigma^{2}}=-\frac{2\pi\,\tau_{1}}{\tau_{2}}(m_{2}+s_{2})~.

(2.43)

In order to allow arbitrary real values for the gauge holonomies, since
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathds{Z}
, we should require
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
to range over

0\leq s_{1}\leq 1\ ,\qquad 0\leq s_{2}\leq 1\ .

(2.44)

In terms of the holomorphic coordinates (
2.36
), eq. (
2.40
) reads

\displaystyle A_{v}

\displaystyle=\partial_{v}X-i\partial_{v}Y-\frac{\bar{u}}{2\tau_{2}}=\partial_{v}X-i\partial_{v}Y-i\partial_{v}\Phi[u]\ ,

(2.45)

\displaystyle A_{\bar{v}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\bar{v}}X+i\partial_{\bar{v}}Y-\frac{u}{2\tau_{2}}=\partial_{\bar{v}}X+i\partial_{\bar{v}}Y+i\partial_{\bar{v}}\Phi[u]\ ,

where we introduced the notation

\Phi[u]=-\sigma_{1}s_{1}+\sigma_{2}s_{2}=\frac{i}{2\tau_{2}}(\bar{v}u-v\bar{u})\ .

(2.46)

In the literature, eq. (
2.45
) is often rewritten as

A_{v}=\partial_{v}\text{a}^{\dagger}\,\text{a}^{\dagger-1}\ ,\qquad A_{\bar{v}}=\partial_{\bar{v}}\text{a}\,\text{a}^{-1}\ ,

(2.47)

where

\text{a}=\Omega\,a\ ,\qquad\text{with}\qquad\Omega=e^{X+iY}\ ,\qquad a=e^{i\Phi[u]}\ .

(2.48)

The advantage of this parametrization of gauge fields is that using eq. (
2.39
), the asymmetric gauged action (
2.19
) can be written as

S^{B}_{i}(g_{i},\text{a},\text{a}^{\dagger})=S^{\text{WZW}}(g_{i})-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\text{Tr}\Biggl[-\varepsilon_{L}^{i}\,\partial_{\bar{v}}\text{a}\,\text{a}^{-1}\,\partial_{v}g_{i}g_{i}^{-1}+\varepsilon_{R}^{i}\,\partial_{v}\text{a}^{\dagger}\,\text{a}^{\dagger-1}\,g_{i}^{-1}\partial_{\bar{v}}g_{i}\\
-\varepsilon_{L}^{i}\,\partial_{\bar{v}}\text{a}\,\text{a}^{-1}\,g_{i}\,\varepsilon_{R}^{i}\,\partial_{v}\text{a}^{\dagger}\,\text{a}^{\dagger-1}\,g_{i}^{-1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{L}^{i}}{2}\,\partial_{\bar{v}}\text{a}\,\text{a}^{-1}\,\varepsilon_{L}^{i}\,\partial_{v}\text{a}^{\dagger}\,\text{a}^{\dagger-1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{R}^{i}}{2}\,\partial_{\bar{v}}\text{a}\,\text{a}^{-1}\,\varepsilon_{R}^{i}\,\partial_{v}\text{a}^{\dagger}\,\text{a}^{\dagger-1}\Biggr]\ .

(2.49)

One can check that using the Polyakov-Wiegmann identity, eq. (
2.49
) takes the concise form

S^{B}_{i}(g_{i},\text{a},\text{a}^{\dagger})=S^{\text{WZW}}(\text{a}^{-\varepsilon^{i}_{L}}g_{i}(\text{a}^{\dagger})^{\varepsilon^{i}_{R}})-S^{\text{WZW}}(\text{a}^{-\varepsilon^{i}_{L}}(\text{a}^{\dagger-1})^{\varepsilon^{i}_{R}})\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\text{Tr}[(\varepsilon^{i}_{L}+\varepsilon^{i}_{R})^{2}]\,\text{a}^{-1}\partial_{\bar{v}}\text{a}\,\partial_{v}\text{a}^{\dagger}\text{a}^{\dagger-1}\ ,

(2.50)

where we adopted the notation

\text{a}^{\varepsilon^{i}_{L}}=e^{(X+iY+i\Phi[u])\varepsilon^{i}_{L}}\ ,\qquad(\text{a}^{\dagger})^{\varepsilon^{i}_{R}}=e^{(X-iY-i\Phi[u])\varepsilon^{i}_{R}}\ ,\qquad(\text{a}^{\dagger-1})^{\varepsilon^{i}_{R}}=e^{(-X+iY+i\Phi[u])\varepsilon^{i}_{R}}\ ,

(2.51)

and so on. We will soon see that writing the asymmetric gauged action as in (
2.50
) makes it easier to decouple the group elements
g_{i}
from the free bosons
X
and
Y
, which enter the parametrization of the gauge field (
2.45
).

We already mentioned that the free fermions entering the action (
2.27
) take value in the “transverse space”
{\mathfrak{g}}_{L}^{{\sf t}}
(respectively
{\mathfrak{g}}_{R}^{{\sf t}}
). The gauge field only couples to charged fermions, so we can split
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sf t}
into the action for fermions in the charged subspace, which will involve the gauge field, and the remaining uncharged fermions, whose action is that of a collection of free fermions and whose partition function is trivial. The action of the fermions is

S^{F}=\int\frac{\text{d}^{2}v}{4\pi}\Bigl(\psi^{A}\bigl(\delta^{AB}\partial_{\bar{v}}-2\text{Tr}(T^{A}[\varepsilon_{L},T^{B}])\,A_{\bar{v}}\bigr)\psi^{B}+\tilde{\psi}^{A}\bigl(\delta^{AB}\partial_{v}-2\text{Tr}(\tilde{T}^{A}[\varepsilon_{R},\tilde{T}^{B}])\,A_{\bar{v}}\bigr)\tilde{\psi}^{B}\Bigr)\ ,

(2.52)

where
A,B
run over the directions in
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sf t}
. Only charged fermions couple to the gauge field. We will suppress explicit mention of the remaining uncharged fermions in the following, while remembering to include their partition function in the final result.

Parametrization of the gauge field for null gauging

When the gauging is null, an alternative approach
Martinec:2020gkv
is to gauge the null currents of
U(1)_{L}
and
U(1)_{R}
using separate gauge fields; one finds that half the components of the two gauge fields simply drop out of the action. The action formally looks like that of the single gauge field formalism we employ here, however the treatment of the zero modes is different – in the Euclidean formalism, the zero modes of
\mathcal{A}_{v}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\bar{v}}
here are complex conjugates of one another, whereas in the treatment of
Martinec:2020gkv
,
A
and
\bar{A}
come from different gauge fields and so their zero modes a priori have nothing to do with one another. As we will see, it is the approach we use here using null gauging with a single gauge field that reproduces known results in the literature. In fact, as we are going to see in the following sections, each gauge field zero mode gives rise to
two
coset constraints of the schematic form
\mathcal{J}_{0}=\bar{\mathcal{J}}_{0}=0
,
where
\mathcal{J}_{0}
and
\bar{\mathcal{J}}_{0}
are the zero modes of respectively the holomorphic and anti-holomorphic currents to be gauged. Treating the gauge fields as in
Martinec:2020gkv
one would then end up with twice as many constraints as the decomposition of the partition function into characters would suggest.

2.4
Decoupling

In the following, we will be interested in evaluating path integrals of the form

Z^{\frac{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{h}}}=\int\mathcal{D}A_{v}\,\mathcal{D}A_{\bar{v}}\,\prod_{i}\int\mathcal{D}g_{i}\prod_{A,\tilde{A}}\int\mathcal{D}\psi_{A}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\psi}_{\tilde{A}}\,e^{-S}\ .

(2.53)

In terms of the parametrization of the gauge field introduced in the previous section we can rewrite eq. (
2.53
) as

Z^{\frac{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{h}}}=\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{1}\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{2}\int\mathcal{D}X\,\mathcal{D}Y\int\mathcal{D}b\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{b}\,\mathcal{D}c\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{c}\,\prod_{i}\int\mathcal{D}g_{i}\prod_{A,\tilde{A}}\int\mathcal{D}\psi_{A}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\psi}_{\tilde{A}}\,e^{-S^{B}-S^{F}-S_{gh}}\ ,

(2.54)

where we introduced the usual
b\,c
ghost action
S_{gh}
, arising from the path integral Jacobian of the transformation
\mathcal{\mathcal{D}}A_{v}\mathcal{D}A_{\bar{v}}\to\mathcal{D}X\mathcal{D}Y
. Assuming invariance of the path integral measure
Gawedzki:1988nj
,

\mathcal{D}g_{i}=\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{\varepsilon_{L}^{i}}\,g_{i}\,\Omega^{\dagger-\varepsilon_{R}^{i}})\ ,\qquad\text{with}\qquad\Omega^{\varepsilon_{L}^{i}}\equiv e^{\varepsilon_{L}^{i}(X+iY)}\ ,\qquad\Omega^{\dagger-\varepsilon_{R}^{i}}\equiv e^{-\varepsilon_{R}^{i}(X-iY)}\ ,

(2.55)

and using eq. (
2.50
) we can replace the action
S^{B}
in the path integral (
2.54
) with

S^{B,d}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{i}^{B}S^{B}_{i}(\Omega^{\varepsilon_{L}^{i}}g_{i}\Omega^{\dagger-\varepsilon_{R}^{i}},\text{a},\text{a}^{\dagger})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\kappa_{i}^{B}S_{g_{i}}+\kappa_{i}^{B}S^{X,Y}_{i})\ ,

(2.56)

where

\displaystyle S_{g_{i}}

\displaystyle=S^{\text{WZW}}(a^{-\varepsilon_{L}^{i}}\,g_{i}\,a^{\dagger\varepsilon_{R}^{i}})\ ,

(2.57a)

\displaystyle S^{X,Y}_{i}

\displaystyle=-S^{\text{WZW}}(a^{-\varepsilon_{L}^{i}}\,\Omega^{-\varepsilon_{L}^{i}}\,\Omega^{\dagger-\varepsilon_{R}^{i}}\,a^{\dagger-\varepsilon_{R}^{i}})-\int\frac{\text{d}^{2}v}{2\pi}\text{Tr}[(\varepsilon_{L}^{i}+\varepsilon_{R}^{i})^{2}]\,|a^{-1}\partial_{\bar{v}}a+\Omega^{-1}\partial_{\bar{v}}\Omega|^{2}\ .

(2.57b)

Notice that in the action (
2.56
) the gauge degrees of freedom
X
and
Y
decoupled from the group valued fields
g_{i}
. This will greatly simplify the computation of partition functions in the following sections.

In a similar spirit, let us see how also the fermions can be decoupled from the gauge degrees of freedom
X
and
Y
. In all the examples we are going to consider, the embeddings take values in the Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
and we can introduce the charges
q_{a}
as

[\varepsilon_{L}^{m},T^{a}_{m}]=q_{a}\,\ell_{m}\,T^{a}_{m}\ ,\qquad[\varepsilon_{R}^{m},\tilde{T}^{a}_{m}]=\tilde{q}_{a}\,r_{m}\,\tilde{T}^{a}_{m}\ ,

(2.58)

where
m
runs over the factors of
{\mathfrak{g}}
containing charged fermions, which we denote by
\psi_{m}^{a}
;
\ell_{m},r_{m}
prescribe the embedding of
\mathfrak{h}
into each factor, and
q_{a},\tilde{q}_{a}
are the weights of the generators
T^{a}_{m},\tilde{T}^{a}_{m}
under those Cartan generators, respectively.
Eq. (
2.52
) thus reduces to

S^{F}=\sum_{m}S^{F}_{m}=\sum_{m}\int\frac{\text{d}^{2}v}{4\pi}\Bigl(\delta^{ab}\psi_{m}^{a}(\partial_{\bar{v}}-q_{b}\,\ell_{m}\,A_{\bar{v}})\psi_{m}^{b}+\delta^{ab}\tilde{\psi}_{m}^{a}(\partial_{v}-\tilde{q}_{b}\,r_{m}\,A_{v})\tilde{\psi}_{m}^{b}\Bigr)\ ,

(2.59)

and is invariant under the gauge transformations

\displaystyle\psi^{a}_{m}

\displaystyle\mapsto e^{q_{a}\,\ell_{m}\,\gamma}\,\psi^{a}_{m}\ ,

\displaystyle\qquad\tilde{\psi}^{a}_{m}

\displaystyle\mapsto e^{\tilde{q}_{a}\,r_{m}\,\gamma}\,\tilde{\psi}^{a}_{m}\ ,

(2.60)

\displaystyle A_{v}

\displaystyle\mapsto A_{v}+\partial_{v}\gamma\ ,

\displaystyle\qquad A_{\bar{v}}

\displaystyle\mapsto A_{\bar{v}}+\partial_{\bar{v}}\gamma\ .

Recall that the gauge fields
A_{v}
,
A_{\bar{v}}
are parametrized as in eq. (
2.45
). Performing a gauge transformation with parameter
\gamma=-X
, we can eliminate
X
from the action (
2.59
) without producing any anomaly factor. We then introduce the fermions

\eta^{a}_{m}=e^{-iq_{a}\ell_{m}(Y+\Phi)}\psi^{a}_{m}\ ,\qquad\tilde{\eta}^{a}_{m}=e^{iq_{a}r_{m}(Y+\Phi)}\tilde{\psi}^{a}_{m}\ .

(2.61)

The fermion measure is not invariant and (
2.61
) gives rise to an anomaly of the form (
2.34
), depending on
Y
,
\Phi
,
\ell_{m}
and
r_{m}
,

\prod_{m}\prod_{a\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sf t}_{m}}\mathcal{D}\psi^{a}_{m}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\psi}^{a}_{m}=\prod_{m}\prod_{a\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sf t}_{m}}\mathcal{D}\eta^{a}_{m}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\eta}^{a}_{m}\,e^{\check{\mathfrak{A}}}\ .

(2.62)

The path integral over the fermions can then be rewritten as

\displaystyle Z^{F}

\displaystyle=\prod_{m}\prod_{a\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sf t}_{m}}\int\mathcal{D}\psi_{m}^{a}\mathcal{D}\tilde{\psi}_{m}^{a}\,e^{-S^{F}}

(2.63)

\displaystyle=e^{\check{\mathfrak{A}}}\prod_{m}\prod_{a\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sf t}_{m}}\int\mathcal{D}\eta_{m}^{a}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\eta}_{m}^{a}\,{\rm exp}\left(\int\frac{\text{d}^{2}v}{4\pi}\delta^{ab}\Bigl(\eta_{m}^{a}\partial_{\bar{v}}\eta_{m}^{b}+\tilde{\eta}_{m}^{a}\partial_{v}\tilde{\eta}_{m}^{b}\Bigr)\right)\ .

(2.64)

Notice that the fermionic degrees of freedom
\eta_{m}^{a}
are now decoupled from the gauge field components
X
and
Y
.

The path-integral (
2.54
) thus factorizes as

Z^{\frac{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{h}}}=\int\mathcal{D}X\,\mathcal{D}Y\,\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{1}\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{2}\,Z_{gh}\,\prod_{i}\,Z^{B}_{i}\prod_{m}\,Z^{F}_{m}\ ,

(2.65)

where the product over
i
runs over the various group factors
G_{i}
of
G
, while the product over
m
runs over the various factors of
{\mathfrak{g}}
with charged fermions, and we introduced the notation

Z^{B}_{i}=e^{-S^{X,Y}_{i}}\int\mathcal{D}g_{i}\,e^{-S_{g_{i}}}\ ,\qquad Z^{F}_{m}=\prod_{a\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{\sf t}_{m}}\int\mathcal{D}\psi_{m}^{a}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\psi}_{m}^{a}\,e^{-S_{m}^{F}}\ .

(2.66)

Finally,

Z_{gh}=\int\mathcal{D}b\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{b}\,\mathcal{D}c\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{c}\,e^{-S_{gh}}=\tau_{2}|\eta(\tau)|^{4}\ .

(2.67)

2.5
Simple gauged WZW models as null gauging

In the following sections we will consider various examples of WZW models
G=G_{L}\times G_{R}
and gauge a null subgroup
H=H_{L}\times H_{R}
with
H_{L}=U(1)
and
H_{R}=U(1)
. The corresponding coset theories are usually denoted as

\frac{G}{H}=\frac{G}{U(1)_{L}\times U(1)_{R}}\ .

(2.68)

In all the examples we are going to encounter, the numerator group
G
contains a non-compact direction, directly involved in the gauging. As a consequence, while the notation (
2.68
) is standard in the literature, strictly speaking the group to be gauged is not
H=U(1)_{L}\times U(1)_{R}
, but rather
H=\mathds{R}\times U(1)
. See
Martinec:2017ztd
;
Martinec:2018nco
;
Martinec:2019wzw
;
Martinec:2020gkv
for a discussion on this point.

We are going to investigate whether standard GWZW models can be equivalently recast in terms of associated
null
GWZW models.
As anticipated in the Introduction, when the gauging of the WZW model is null, the presence of the additional symmetry (
2.32
) suggests that the standard GWZW
G/H
can schematically be recast as

\frac{G}{H}\simeq\frac{G\times\tilde{H}}{U(1)_{L}\times U(1)_{R}}\ ,

(2.69)

where
U(1)_{L}
and
U(1)_{R}
are null isometries involving
\tilde{H}
. One of our goals is to make eq. (
2.69
) precise in various examples and check whether the partition functions of the models in the LHS and RHS of (
2.69
) agree. This equivalence is not obvious since the naive argument discussed above and leading to eq. (
2.69
) does not take into account gauge field zero modes. We begin in Section
3
by showing that the partition function of the
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric coset

\frac{SU(2)}{U(1)_{V,A}}

(2.70)

agrees with the partition function of the null gauged model

\frac{{SU(2)}\times\mathds{R}}{U(1)_{L}\times U(1)_{R}}\ .

(2.71)

In (
2.70
) the subscript
V,A
stands for “vector” or “axial”. The distinction between these two options here is not important as the partition functions of these two models agree
Kac:1984mq
;
Fateev:1985mm
;
Gepner:1986hr
;
Huitu:1990xv
. Beyond (
2.71
) having an extra factor in the numerator group, the main a priori difference between the GWZW models (
2.70
) and (
2.71
) is the following. While in eq. (
2.70
) the non-gauge part of
A_{v}
and
A_{\bar{v}}
is a physical field
Y
, see eq. (
2.45
), in eq. (
2.71
) all of the gauge field is pure gauge, apart from zero modes. Although it is somewhat non-trivial that the two are the same, to some extent we are just relabelling the
Y
field from parametrizing the gauge field to parametrizing an extra component of the upstairs group. Similar statements may be formulated for the putative equivalence

\frac{SL(2,\mathds{R})}{U(1)_{A}}\simeq\frac{SL(2,\mathds{R})\times U(1)}{U(1)_{L}\times U(1)_{R}}\ ,

(2.72)

which is analyzed in Section
4
. Things become more nontrivial when we get to product groups like
G=SL(2,\mathds{R})\times{SU(2)}
and beyond. In fact, in
Israel:2004ir
equivalence of the supersymmetric GWZW models

\left(\frac{SL(2,\mathds{R})_{n_{5}}}{U(1)_{A}}\times\frac{SU(2)_{n_{5}}}{U(1)_{V}}\right)\!\bigg/\mathds{Z}_{n_{5}}\simeq\frac{SL(2,\mathds{R})_{n_{5}}\times SU(2)_{n_{5}}}{U(1)_{L}\times U(1)_{R}}

(2.73)

was argued.
This equivalence has been confirmed by a more detailed study of the spectrum in
Martinec:2018nco
. In Section
5
, we compute the partition function of the null gauged model in the RHS of eq. (
2.73
) and show it indeed reproduces the partition function of the coset orbifold on the LHS.

3
Warmup:
\bm{\frac{{SU(2)}}{U(1)}}

In preparation for Sections
5
and
6
, let us warm up with one of the best known coset CFT’s, the
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric GWZW model

\frac{SU(2)_{\kappa_{su}}}{U(1)}\ .

(3.1)

The distinction between axial and vector gauging is not important here, since the partition function does not depend on this choice. The partition function is well-known
Kac:1984mq
;
Fateev:1985mm
;
Gepner:1986hr
;
Huitu:1990xv
and for the reader’s convenience it is reviewed in Appendix
B
. As is typical of supersymmetric theories, the partition function depends on whether we are working in the NS or R sector and on whether or not we consider insertions of
(-1)^{F_{L}+F_{R}}
operators, where
F_{L}
and
F_{R}
count the number of worldsheet fermions in the holomorphic and anti-holomorphic sector respectively. To be concrete, we will consider the partition function

\mathcal{Z}^{\frac{\mathfrak{su}(2)}{\mathfrak{u}(1)}}(\tau,z)=\text{Tr}_{\text{R}}[(-1)^{F_{L}+F_{R}}q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\,\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\,e^{2\pi iz(J^{\mathcal{R}}_{su})_{0}}\,e^{-2\pi i\bar{z}\bar{(}J^{\mathcal{R}}_{su})_{0}}]\ ,

(3.2)

with the trace taken over the worldsheet R-R sector. The computation we are going to present is completely analogous for the other sectors. The current
J^{\mathcal{R}}_{su}+\bar{J}^{\mathcal{R}}_{su}
entering eq. (
3.2
) is the
\mathcal{R}
-current of the
\mathcal{N}=2
model (
3.1
). The
\mathcal{R}
-current
J^{\mathcal{R}}_{su}
and the
\mathcal{N}=1
Cartan
J^{3}_{su}
of the
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{\kappa_{su}}
WZW model can be written as the sum of the bosonic and fermionic Cartans
j^{3}_{su}
and
j^{3}_{f,su}
,

J^{3}_{su}=j^{3}_{su}+j^{3}_{f,su}\ ,\qquad J^{\mathcal{R}}_{su}=-\frac{2}{\kappa_{su}}j^{3}_{su}+\frac{\kappa_{su}-2}{\kappa_{su}}j^{3}_{f,su}\ ,

(3.3)

where the bosonic level
\kappa_{su}^{B}
is related to the supersymmetric level
\kappa_{su}
as

\kappa_{su}=\kappa^{B}_{su}+2\ .

(3.4)

We associate chemical potentials to each current according to Table
1
and similarly for the anti-holomorphic sector.

Current

j^{3}_{su}

j^{3}_{f,su}

J^{3}_{su}

J^{\mathcal{R}}_{su}

Chemical potential

z_{B}

z_{F}

u

z

Table 1
:
We list various currents together with the associated chemical potentials.

Requiring that

z\,J^{\mathcal{R}}_{su}+u\,J^{3}_{su}=z_{B}\,j^{3}_{su}+z_{F}\,j^{3}_{f,su}\ ,

(3.5)

we find that the chemical potentials are related by

z_{B}=u-\frac{2}{\kappa_{su}}z\ ,\qquad z_{F}=u+\frac{\kappa_{su}-2}{\kappa_{su}}z\ .

(3.6)

Following the discussion in Section
2.5
, we are going to show that the partition function of the coset theory (
3.1
) exactly matches the one derived from the null gauged coset
7
7

7

A similar computation has been briefly sketched in
Gawedzki:1991yu
for the bosonic parafermion theory.

\frac{SU(2)\times{\mathds{R}}_{t}}{U(1)_{L}\times U(1)_{R}}\ ,

(3.7)

where
\mathds{R}_{t}
is a non-compact time-like free boson while
U(1)_{L}
and
U(1)_{R}
are parameterized by the embeddings

\varepsilon_{L}^{su}=\varepsilon_{R}^{su}=-\frac{\sigma_{3}}{2}\ ,\qquad\varepsilon_{L}^{t}=-\varepsilon_{R}^{t}=-\frac{\sqrt{\kappa_{su}}}{2}\ .

(3.8)

As anticipated in Section
2.5
, the non-compact direction
\mathds{R}_{t}
enters the gauging in eq. (
3.7
) and the denominator should be more appropriately described as
U(1)\times\mathds{R}
. Formally, we will model the
\mathds{R}_{t}
time-like free boson as a WZW model at level
\kappa_{t}=-2
with group element

g_{t}=e^{it}

(3.9)

and OPE

\partial_{v}t(v_{1})\,\partial_{v}t(v_{2})=\frac{1}{2\,(v_{1}-v_{2})^{2}}\ .

(3.10)

Notice that the gauging (
3.8
) is null,

\sum_{i}\kappa_{i}\text{Tr}[(\varepsilon_{L}^{i})^{2}]=\sum_{i}\kappa_{i}\text{Tr}[(\varepsilon_{R}^{i})^{2}]=\kappa_{su}\,\text{Tr}\left[\left(\frac{\sigma_{3}}{2}\right)^{2}\right]-2\left(\tfrac{\sqrt{\kappa_{su}}}{2}\right)^{2}=0\ .

(3.11)

Since the chemical potentials
z_{B}
and
z_{F}
, associated respectively to the currents
j^{3}_{su}
and
j^{3}_{f,su}
, are independent and not related to one another, one should in principle have two gauge fields: one associated to the bosonic current
j^{3}_{su}
and a second one associated to the fermionic current
j^{3}_{f,su}
. Having the same gauge field for
both
the bosonic and the fermionic currents amounts to having a unique chemical potential
u
associated to
J^{3}_{su}=j^{3}_{su}+j^{3}_{f,su}
and no chemical potential associated to the orthogonal current
J^{\mathcal{R}}_{su}
. This is implicitly what we did at the level of the general discussion of Section
2
. On the other hand, here we are interested in keeping track of the
\mathcal{R}
-current quantum numbers, associated to the chemical potential
z
. Following
Eguchi:2010cb
;
Eguchi:2004yi
, we will do so by evaluating the bosonic and fermionic
SU(2)
partition functions (
2.66
) respectively at the bosonic and fermionic chemical potentials
z_{B}
and
z_{F}
. Using eq. (
3.6
), it is then easy to recover the dependence of the partition function (
2.65
) on the chemical potentials
z
and
u
. The partition function of the
\mathcal{N}=2
coset (
3.1
) thus reads,
8
8

8

We will see momentarily that the fields
X
and
Y
decouple completely. We then omit the infinite volume factor arising from path integrating over these fields,
i.e.
we “factor out the gauge group”.

Z^{\frac{\mathfrak{su}(2)}{\mathfrak{u}(1)}}(\tau,z)=\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{1}\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{2}\,Z_{gh}(\tau)\,Z^{B}_{su}(\tau,z_{B})\,Z^{B}_{t}(\tau,u)\,Z^{F}_{su}(\tau,z_{F})\ .

(3.12)

As discussed in Section
2.4
and explained in detail in Appendix
E
, when the gauging is null, worldsheet fermions take value in the adjoint representation of the
transverse
space
\mathfrak{g}^{\sf t}
to the gauge direction in the Lie algebra, see eq. (
E.70
). In our case, this means only the fermions
\psi^{+}_{su}
and
\psi^{-}_{su}
(together with the anti-holomorphic fields
\tilde{\psi}^{+}_{su}
and
\tilde{\psi}^{-}_{su}
) enter the partition function (
3.12
). Let us now derive the various terms entering eq. (
3.12
).

3.1
Bosonic sector

In this section we evaluate the bosonic contributions
Z^{B}_{su}(\tau,z_{B})
and
Z^{B}_{t}(\tau,u)
entering eq. (
3.12
). We begin with
Z^{B}_{su}(\tau,z_{B})
. Using eq. (
D.14
) we find

\int\mathcal{D}g_{su}\,{\rm exp}\left(-\kappa^{B}_{su}\,S_{g_{su}}\right)=\int\mathcal{D}g_{su}\,{\rm exp}\left(-\kappa^{B}_{su}\,S^{\text{WZW}}\bigl(a[z_{B}]^{-\varepsilon_{L}^{su}}\,g_{su}\,a[z_{B}]^{\dagger\varepsilon_{L}^{su}}\bigr)\right)\\
={\rm exp}\left(\frac{\pi\,(\kappa_{su}-2)}{4\tau_{2}}\,(z_{B}-\bar{z}_{B})^{2}\right)\mathcal{Z}_{B}^{\mathfrak{su}(2)}(\tau,z_{B})\ ,

(3.13)

where
\mathcal{Z}_{B}^{\mathfrak{su}(2)}
is the trace partition function of the bosonic
\mathfrak{su}(2)
model at level
\kappa^{B}_{su}=\kappa_{su}-2
, see eq. (
B.2
). For
S_{su}^{X,Y}
we find

S_{su}^{X,Y}=-\frac{\kappa_{su}-2}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\big|\partial_{v}(Y+\Phi[z_{B}])\big|^{2}\ ,

(3.14)

and hence using eq. (
2.66
),

Z^{B}_{su}(\tau,z_{B})={\rm exp}\left(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\pi\, (\kappa_{su}-2)}{4\tau_2}$}}\,(z_{B}-\bar{z}_{B})^{2}+\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\kappa_{su}-2}{\pi}$}}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\big|\partial_{v}(Y+\Phi[z_{B}])\big|^{2}\right)\mathcal{Z}_{B}^{\mathfrak{su}(2)}(\tau,z_{B})\ .

(3.15)

Let us now consider
Z^{B}_{t}(\tau,u)
. The actions
S_{g_{t}}
and
S^{X,Y}_{t}
read, see eq. (
2.57
)

\displaystyle S_{g_{t}}=\kappa_{t}\,S^{\text{WZW}}\bigl(a[u]^{-\varepsilon_{L}^{t}}\,g_{t}\,a[u]^{\dagger\varepsilon_{R}^{t}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,|\partial_{v}t|^{2}\ ,

(3.16)

\displaystyle S^{X,Y}_{t}=-\kappa_{t}\,S^{\text{WZW}}\bigl(a[u]^{-\varepsilon_{L}^{t}}\,\Omega^{-\varepsilon_{L}^{t}}\,\Omega^{\dagger-\varepsilon_{R}^{t}}\,a[u]^{\dagger-\varepsilon_{R}^{t}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{su}}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\big|\partial_{v}(Y+\Phi[u])\big|^{2}\ ,

(3.17)

and hence

Z^{B}_{t}(\tau,u)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tau_{2}}|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}\,{\rm exp}\left(-\frac{\kappa_{su}}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\big|\partial_{v}(Y+\Phi[u])\big|^{2}\right)\ .

(3.18)

Collecting the various terms, we find

Z^{B}_{su}(\tau,z_{B})\,Z^{B}_{t}(\tau,u)=\frac{{\rm exp}\left(\frac{\pi\,(\kappa_{su}-2)}{4\tau_{2}}(z_{B}-\bar{z}_{B})^{2}\right)}{\sqrt{\tau_{2}}|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}\,\sum_{j=0}^{\kappa_{su}-2}\chi_{j,B}^{\mathfrak{su}(2)}(\tau,z_{B})\,\overline{\chi_{j,B}^{\mathfrak{su}(2)}(\tau,z_{B})}\\
\times\,{\rm exp}\left(\frac{\kappa_{su}-2}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,|\partial_{v}(Y+\Phi[z_{B}])|^{2}-\frac{\kappa_{su}}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\big|\partial_{v}(Y+\Phi[u])\big|^{2}\right)\ ,

(3.19)

where we used eq. (
B.2
) to express the
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{\kappa_{su}-2}
trace partition function in terms of characters.

3.2
Fermionic sector

Following
Henningson:1993nr
;
Eguchi:2004yi
;
Eguchi:2010cb
;
Ashok:2011cy
let us explain how to compute the
SU(2)
fermionic partition function
9
9

9

The reduction of the initial path integral involving the four fermions
\psi^{\pm}_{su},\psi^{3}_{su},\psi^{t}
to those spanning the transverse space to the gauge deformations (namely
\psi^{\pm}_{su}
) is discussed in Appendix
E
.

Z^{F}_{su}(\tau,z_{F})=\int\mathcal{D}\psi^{+}_{su}\,\mathcal{D}\psi^{-}_{su}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\psi}^{+}_{su}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\psi}^{-}_{su}\,e^{-S_{su}^{F}}\ ,

(3.20)

where

S_{su}^{F}=\int\frac{\text{d}^{2}v}{2\pi}\left[\psi_{su}^{+}(\partial_{\bar{v}}+A_{\bar{v}})\psi^{-}_{su}+\psi^{-}_{su}(\partial_{\bar{v}}-A_{\bar{v}})\psi^{+}_{su}+\tilde{\psi}^{+}_{su}(\partial_{v}+A_{v})\tilde{\psi}^{-}_{su}+\tilde{\psi}^{-}_{su}(\partial_{v}-A_{v})\tilde{\psi}^{+}_{su}\right]\ .

(3.21)

The action (
3.21
) is gauge invariant under

\displaystyle\psi^{\pm}_{su}\mapsto e^{\pm\gamma}\psi^{\pm}_{su}

\displaystyle,\qquad A_{v}\mapsto A_{v}+\partial_{v}\gamma~~,

(3.22)

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}^{\pm}_{su}\mapsto e^{\pm\gamma}\tilde{\psi}^{\pm}_{su}

\displaystyle,\qquad A_{\bar{v}}\mapsto A_{\bar{v}}+\partial_{\bar{v}}\gamma~~.

In the parametrization (
2.45
) of the gauge field, we can eliminate
X
from (
3.21
) by a gauge transformation with gauge parameter
\gamma=-X
. Let us now introduce the fermions

\displaystyle\eta^{+}_{su}=e^{-iY-i\Phi}\psi^{+}_{su}

\displaystyle,\qquad\tilde{\eta}^{+}_{su}=e^{+iY+i\Phi}\tilde{\psi}^{+}_{su}~~,

(3.23)

\displaystyle\eta^{-}_{su}=e^{+iY+i\Phi}\psi^{-}_{su}

\displaystyle,\qquad\tilde{\eta}^{-}_{su}=e^{-iY-i\Phi}\tilde{\psi}^{-}_{su}~~,

in terms of which the action (
3.21
) becomes

S_{su}^{F}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\left[\eta^{+}_{su}\partial_{\bar{v}}\eta^{-}_{su}+\eta^{-}_{su}\partial_{\bar{v}}\eta^{+}_{su}+\tilde{\eta}^{+}_{su}\partial_{v}\tilde{\eta}^{-}_{su}+\tilde{\eta}^{-}_{su}\partial_{v}\tilde{\eta}^{+}_{su}\right]\ .

(3.24)

As anticipated in Section
2
, the “rotation” (
3.23
) is anomalous and produces the anomaly factor

\mathcal{D}\psi^{+}_{su}\,\mathcal{D}\psi^{-}_{su}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\psi}^{+}_{su}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\psi}^{-}_{su}=\mathcal{D}\eta^{+}\,\mathcal{D}\eta^{-}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\eta}^{+}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\eta}^{-}\,e^{\check{\mathfrak{A}}}\ ,

(3.25)

where for a chiral rotation of the form

\begin{pmatrix}\delta\psi^{\pm}_{su}\\
\delta\tilde{\psi}^{\pm}_{su}\end{pmatrix}=\pm i\,\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&-\alpha\end{pmatrix}\,\begin{pmatrix}\psi^{\pm}_{su}\\
\tilde{\psi}^{\pm}_{su}\end{pmatrix}

(3.26)

the anomaly reads
Weinberg:1996kr

\check{\mathfrak{A}}=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\text{d}x^{0}\text{d}x^{1}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}\,\partial_{\mu}\alpha\,A_{\nu}=\frac{2}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\partial_{v}(Y+\Phi[z_{F}])\,\partial_{\bar{v}}(Y+\Phi[z_{F}])\ .

(3.27)

In the second equality in (
3.27
) we used that

\alpha=-Y-\Phi[z_{F}]\ ,\qquad A_{v}=-i\partial_{v}Y-i\partial_{v}\Phi[z_{F}]\ ,\qquad A_{\bar{v}}=i\partial_{\bar{v}}Y+i\partial_{\bar{v}}\Phi[z_{F}]\ .

(3.28)

The path integral (
3.20
) then becomes

Z^{F}_{su}(\tau,z_{F})={\rm exp}\left(\frac{2}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\partial_{v}(Y+\Phi[z_{F}])\,\partial_{\bar{v}}(Y+\Phi[z_{F}])\right)\,Z^{\eta}_{su}\ ,

(3.29)

with

Z^{\eta}_{su}=\int\mathcal{D}\eta^{+}_{su}\mathcal{D}\eta^{-}_{su}\mathcal{D}\tilde{\eta}^{+}_{su}\mathcal{D}\tilde{\eta}^{-}_{su}\,{\rm exp}\left(-\int\frac{\text{d}^{2}v}{2\pi}\left[\eta^{+}_{su}\partial_{\bar{v}}\eta^{-}_{su}+\eta^{-}_{su}\partial_{\bar{v}}\eta^{+}_{su}+\tilde{\eta}^{+}_{su}\partial_{v}\tilde{\eta}^{-}_{su}+\tilde{\eta}^{-}_{su}\partial_{v}\tilde{\eta}^{+}_{su}\right]\right)\ .

(3.30)

It remains to compute the partition function
Z^{\eta}_{su}
. Notice that while the fermions
\psi^{\pm}_{su},\tilde{\psi}^{\pm}_{su}
have periodic boundary conditions (we are in the
\tilde{\text{R}}
-sector,
i.e.
the
R
-sector with additional insertions of
(-1)^{F}
), the fermions
\eta^{\pm}_{su},\tilde{\eta}^{\pm}_{su}
obey

\displaystyle\eta^{\pm}_{su}(v+2\pi,\bar{v}+2\pi)

\displaystyle=e^{\pm 2\pi is_{1}^{\prime}}\eta^{\pm}_{su}(v,\bar{v})\ ,

(3.31)

\displaystyle\eta^{\pm}_{su}(v+2\pi\tau,\bar{v}+2\pi\bar{\tau})

\displaystyle=e^{\mp 2\pi is_{2}^{\prime}}\eta^{\pm}_{su}(v,\bar{v})\ ,

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}^{\pm}_{su}(v+2\pi,\bar{v}+2\pi)

\displaystyle=e^{\mp 2\pi is_{1}^{\prime}}\tilde{\eta}^{\pm}_{su}(v,\bar{v})\ ,

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}^{\pm}_{su}(v+2\pi\tau,\bar{v}+2\pi\bar{\tau})

\displaystyle=e^{\pm 2\pi is_{2}^{\prime}}\tilde{\eta}^{\pm}_{su}(v,\bar{v})\ ,

where we defined
s_{1}^{\prime}
and
s_{2}^{\prime}
by

z_{F}=s_{1}^{\prime}\tau+s_{2}^{\prime}\ .

(3.32)

The partition function of free fermions with arbitrary boundary conditions has been computed in
Alvarez-Gaume:1986rcs
. Exploiting their findings and using eq. (
3.31
) we obtain

Z^{\eta}_{su}=\Bigl|q^{-\frac{1}{24}}q^{\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2})}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n+s_{1}^{\prime}}e^{2\pi is_{2}^{\prime}})(1-q^{n-s_{1}^{\prime}-1}e^{-2\pi is_{2}^{\prime}})\Bigr|^{2}=e^{\frac{\pi}{2\tau_{2}}\,(z_{F}-\bar{z}_{F})^{2}}\frac{|\theta_{1}(\tau,z_{F})|^{2}}{|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}\ ,

(3.33)

see Appendix
A
for the definitions of Theta functions. All together, the partition function (
3.20
) reads

Z^{F}_{su}(\tau,z_{F})=\frac{{\rm exp}\left(\frac{\pi}{2\tau_{2}}\,(z_{F}-\bar{z}_{F})^{2}\right)}{|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}\,|\theta_{1}(\tau,z_{F})|^{2}\,{\rm exp}\left(\frac{2}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\big|\partial_{v}(Y+\Phi[z_{F}])\big|^{2}\right)\ .

(3.34)

3.3
The coset partition function

The coset partition function follows directly from eqs. (
2.67
), (
3.12
), (
3.19
) and (
3.34
),

Z^{\frac{\mathfrak{su}(2)}{\mathfrak{u}(1)}}(\tau,z)=\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\,e^{\frac{2\pi}{\tau_{2}}\frac{\kappa_{su}-2}{\kappa_{su}}z_{1}^{2}}\,\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{1}\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{2}\,e^{-\frac{\pi\kappa_{su}}{\tau_{2}}u_{2}^{2}}\,\left|\theta_{1}(\tau,z_{F})\right|^{2}\,\sum_{j=0}^{\kappa_{su}-2}\left|\chi_{j,B}^{\mathfrak{su}(2)}(\tau,z_{B})\right|^{2}\ .

(3.35)

Let us now see how evaluating the integral over
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
we will recover the trace partition function (
B.21
). We first relate the characters of the bosonic
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{\kappa_{su}-2}
to the characters
I^{j}_{\ell}
of the
\mathcal{N}=2
coset model (
3.1
) and from eq. (
B.17
) we obtain

Z^{\frac{\mathfrak{su}(2)}{\mathfrak{u}(1)}}(\tau,z)=\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\,{\rm exp}\left(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2 \pi}{\tau_2}$}}\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\kappa_{su}-2}{\kappa_{su}}$}}z_{1}^{2}\right)\,\sum_{j=0}^{\kappa_{su}-2}\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}=-\kappa_{su}+1}^{\kappa_{su}}I^{j}_{\ell}(\tau,z)\,\overline{I^{j}_{\ell^{\prime}}(\tau,z)}\\
\times\,\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{1}\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{2}\,{\rm exp}\left(-\frac{\pi\kappa_{su}}{\tau_{2}}u_{2}^{2}\right)\,\Theta_{\ell}^{(\kappa_{su})}(\tau,u)\,\overline{\Theta_{\ell^{\prime}}^{(\kappa_{su})}(\tau,u)}~.\hskip 14.22636pt~

(3.36)

See Appendices
A
and
B
for the precise definition of the various quantities entering eq. (
3.36
). Orthogonality of the
\Theta_{\ell}^{(k)}(\tau,u)
functions, see eq. (
A.10
), implies

Z^{\frac{\mathfrak{su}(2)}{\mathfrak{u}(1)}}(\tau,z)=\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\,{\rm exp}\left(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2 \pi}{\tau_2}$}}\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\kappa_{su}-2}{\kappa_{su}}$}}z_{1}^{2}\right)\,\sum_{j=0}^{\kappa_{su}-2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\kappa_{su}}|I_{\ell}^{j}(\tau,z)|^{2}\\
\times\,\sum_{n\in\mathds{Z}}\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{1}\left(e^{-{\pi\kappa_{su}}{\tau_{2}}\left(s_{1}+2n+\frac{\ell}{\kappa_{su}}\right)^{2}}+e^{-{\pi\kappa_{su}}{\tau_{2}}\left(s_{1}+2n-1+\frac{\ell}{\kappa_{su}}\right)^{2}}\right)\ ,

(3.37)

where we have split the sum over
\ell
, used eq. (
B.19
) and renamed the summation labels
j\mapsto\kappa_{su}-2-j
and
\ell\mapsto\ell-\kappa_{su}
. We can then trade the sum over
n
and the integral over
s_{1}
for an integral over the real line,

\displaystyle Z^{\frac{\mathfrak{su}(2)}{\mathfrak{u}(1)}}(\tau,z)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\,{\rm exp}\left(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2 \pi}{\tau_2}$}}\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\kappa_{su}-2}{\kappa_{su}}$}}z_{1}^{2}\right)\,\sum_{j=0}^{\kappa_{su}-2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\kappa_{su}}|I_{\ell}^{j}(\tau,z)|^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\text{d}s_{1}e^{-{\pi\kappa_{su}}{\tau_{2}}(s_{1}+\tfrac{\ell}{\kappa_{su}})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm exp}\left(\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{2 \pi}{\tau_2}$}}\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\kappa_{su}-2}{\kappa_{su}}$}}z_{1}^{2}\right)}{\sqrt{\kappa_{su}}}\,\sum_{j=0}^{\kappa_{su}-2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\kappa_{su}}|I_{\ell}^{j}(\tau,z)|^{2}\ .

(3.38)

This does not yet look like the expected result, compare with eq. (
B.21
). Let us then double the sum over
\ell
, use the character identity (
B.19
) and rename the sum label as
\ell\mapsto\ell+\kappa_{su}
,

\displaystyle Z^{\frac{\mathfrak{su}(2)}{\mathfrak{u}(1)}}(\tau,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm exp}\left(\frac{2\pi}{\tau_{2}}\frac{\kappa_{su}-2}{\kappa_{su}}z_{1}^{2}\right)}{2\sqrt{\kappa_{su}}}\,\sum_{j=0}^{\kappa_{su}-2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\kappa_{su}}\left(|I_{\ell}^{j}(\tau,z)|^{2}+|I_{\ell-\kappa}^{j}(\tau,z)|^{2}\right)

(3.39)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm exp}\left(\frac{2\pi}{\tau_{2}}\frac{\kappa_{su}-2}{\kappa_{su}}z_{1}^{2}\right)}{2\sqrt{\kappa_{su}}}\sum_{j=0}^{\kappa_{su}-2}\sum_{\ell=1-\kappa}^{\kappa_{su}}|I_{\ell}^{j}(\tau,z)|^{2}\ .

(3.40)

The path integral partition function (
3.40
) agrees with the trace partition function (
B.21
) up to the multiplicative factor

\frac{{\rm exp}\left(\frac{2\pi}{\tau_{2}}\frac{\kappa_{su}-2}{\kappa_{su}}z_{1}^{2}\right)}{\sqrt{\kappa}_{su}}\ .

(3.41)

The factor of
\sqrt{\kappa}_{su}
in the denominator can be absorbed in the overall normalisation, which still has to be fixed. The exponential factor is of the form

{\rm exp}\left(\frac{2\pi}{\tau_{2}}\hat{c}\,z_{1}^{2}\right)\ ,\qquad\text{with}\qquad\hat{c}=\frac{c}{3}=\frac{\kappa_{su}-2}{\kappa_{su}}\ .

(3.42)

This is the expected relative factor relating the
path integral
partition function to the
trace
partition function, see Appendix
D
where following
Kraus:2006nb
we recap its origin.

4
Warmup:
\bm{\frac{SL(2,\mathds{R})}{U(1)}}

Let us consider a further warmup example, the
\mathcal{N}=2
“cigar” theory defined by the axial coset
Witten:1991yr
;
Dijkgraaf:1991ba
;
Gawedzki:1991yu

\frac{SL(2,\mathds{R})_{\kappa_{sl}}}{U(1)_{A}}\ .

(4.1)

The path integral partition function

Z^{\frac{\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathds{R})}{\mathfrak{u}(1)}}(\tau,z)=\text{Tr}_{R}\Big[(-1)^{F_{L}+F_{R}}q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\,\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\,e^{2\pi izJ^{\mathcal{R}}_{sl}}\,e^{-2\pi i\bar{z}\bar{J}^{\mathcal{R}}_{sl}}\Big]

(4.2)

of the supersymmetric cigar theory has been computed in
Eguchi:2004yi
;
Israel:2004ir
;
Eguchi:2010cb
. To strengthen the claims of Section
2.5
, we are going to rederive it in terms of the null gauged coset
10
10

10

Strictly speaking, in order to compute the path integral, we will consider the analytic continuation of
SL(2,\mathds{R})
to
H_{3}^{+}
, see
e.g.

Gawedzki:1988nj
;
Gawedzki:1991yu
;
Maldacena:2000kv
;
Hanany:2002ev
;
Eguchi:2004yi
for more details on this.

\frac{SL(2,\mathds{R})_{\kappa_{sl}}\times U(1)_{y}}{U(1)_{L}\times U(1)_{R}}\ ,

(4.3)

with embeddings

\varepsilon_{L}^{sl}=-\varepsilon_{R}^{sl}=-\frac{\sigma_{3}}{2}\ ,\qquad\varepsilon_{L}^{y}=\varepsilon_{R}^{y}=\frac{\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}}{2}\ .

(4.4)

Similarly to Section
3
, the currents
J^{3}_{sl}
and
J^{\mathcal{R}}_{sl}
can be written as

J^{3}_{sl}=j^{3}_{sl}+j^{3}_{f,sl}\ ,\qquad J^{\mathcal{R}}_{sl}=\frac{2}{\kappa_{sl}}j^{3}_{sl}+\frac{\kappa_{sl}+2}{\kappa_{sl}}j^{3}_{f,sl}\ ,

(4.5)

and chemical potentials
u
and
z
associated respectively to
J^{3}_{sl}
and
J^{\mathcal{R}}_{sl}
are related to the bosonic and fermionic chemical potentials
z_{B}
and
z_{F}
by

z_{B}=u+\frac{2}{\kappa_{sl}}z\ ,\qquad z_{F}=u+\frac{\kappa_{sl}+2}{\kappa_{sl}}z\ .

(4.6)

We parameterize the gauge field entering the decoupled action (
2.56
),

\displaystyle S^{B,d}=S_{g_{sl}}+S_{g_{y}}+S^{X,Y}_{sl}+S^{X,Y}_{y}\ ,

(4.7)

as discussed in Section
2.3
. The path integral factorizes as

Z^{\frac{\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathds{R})}{\mathfrak{u}(1)}}(\tau,z)=\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{1}\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{2}\,Z_{gh}(\tau)\,Z^{B}_{sl}(\tau,z_{B})\,Z^{B}_{y}(\tau,u)\,Z^{F}_{sl}(\tau,z_{F})\ .

(4.8)

Also in this case, since the gauging is null, the only fermions entering the action are those taking values in the “transverse space”,
i.e.

\psi^{\pm}_{sl}
and
\tilde{\psi}^{\pm}_{sl}
(again, see Appendix
E
).
Formally, we model the free compact boson
y
with radius

R=\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}

(4.9)

as a
U(1)
WZW model at level 2, with group element

g_{y}=e^{iy}

(4.10)

and OPE

\partial_{v}y(z)\,\partial_{v}y(w)=-\frac{1}{2(z-w)^{2}}\ .

(4.11)

With these conventions, it is easy to check that the gauging is indeed null,
11
11

11

The WZW action for
SL(2,\mathds{R})
has an additional minus sign relative to (
2.1
) so that the metric has the correct signature, see
e.g.

Eguchi:2010cb
.

\sum_{i}\kappa_{i}\text{Tr}[(\varepsilon_{L}^{i})^{2}]=\sum_{i}\kappa_{i}\text{Tr}[(\varepsilon_{R}^{i})^{2}]=-\kappa_{sl}\,\text{Tr}\left[\left(\frac{\sigma_{3}}{2}\right)^{2}\right]+2\Big(\frac{\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}}{2}\Big)^{2}=0\ .

(4.12)

4.1
Bosonic sector

Let us compute
Z^{B}_{sl}(\tau,z_{B})
and
Z^{B}_{y}(\tau,u)
. We begin with
Z^{B}_{sl}(\tau,z_{B})
and consider

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{D}g_{sl}\,e^{-S_{g_{sl}}}

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}g_{sl}\,{\rm exp}\left[-S^{\text{WZW}}\left(a[z_{B}]^{-\varepsilon_{L}^{sl}}\,g_{sl}\,a[z_{B}]^{\dagger\varepsilon_{R}^{sl}}\right)+S^{\text{WZW}}(a[z_{B}]^{\varepsilon_{R}^{sl}}\,a[z_{B}]^{\dagger\varepsilon_{R}^{sl}})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-\pi\frac{(z_{B}-\bar{z}_{B})^{2}}{2\tau_{2}}}}{\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\,|\theta_{1}(\tau,z_{B})|^{2}}\ ,

(4.13)

where in the first equality we used that

S^{\text{WZW}}\left(a[u]^{\varepsilon_{R}^{sl}}\,a[u]^{\dagger\varepsilon_{R}^{sl}}\right)=0\ ,

(4.14)

while in the second one we recognised the path integral partition function of the bosonic
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathds{R})
WZW model
Gawedzki:1988nj
;
Gawedzki:1991yu
;
Eguchi:2010cb
,
12
12

12

Eq. (
4.15
) is correct up to some normalization constant. However, this will not be relevant for us.

Z^{GK}_{sl}(\tau,z_{B})=\frac{e^{-\pi\frac{(z_{B}-\bar{z}_{B})^{2}}{2\tau_{2}}}}{\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\,|\theta_{1}(\tau,z_{B})|^{2}}\ .

(4.15)

For
S^{X,Y}_{sl}
we find

\displaystyle S^{X,Y}_{sl}

\displaystyle=-S^{\text{WZW}}(a[z_{B}]^{-\varepsilon_{L}^{sl}}\Omega^{-\varepsilon_{L}^{sl}}\Omega^{\dagger-\varepsilon_{R}^{sl}}a[z_{B}]^{\dagger-\varepsilon_{R}^{sl}})=\frac{\kappa_{sl}+2}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\big|\partial_{v}(Y+\Phi[z_{B}])\big|^{2}\ ,

(4.16)

The two
U(1)_{y}
terms in (
4.7
) read

S_{g_{y}}=S^{\text{WZW}}\left(a[u]^{-\varepsilon_{L}^{y}}\,g_{y}\,a[u]^{\dagger\varepsilon_{R}^{y}}\right)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\partial_{v}(y+\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}\Phi[u])\,\partial_{\bar{v}}(y+\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}\Phi[u])\ ,

(4.17)

and

\displaystyle S^{X,Y}_{y}

\displaystyle=-S^{\text{WZW}}(a[u]^{-\varepsilon_{L}^{y}}\Omega^{-\varepsilon_{L}^{y}}\Omega^{\dagger-\varepsilon_{R}^{y}}a[u]^{\dagger-\varepsilon_{R}^{y}})-\frac{2}{2\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,(\varepsilon_{L}^{y}+\varepsilon_{R}^{y})^{2}\,|a[u]^{-1}\partial_{\bar{v}}a[u]+\Omega^{-1}\partial_{\bar{v}}\Omega|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{sl}}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\partial_{v}X\,\partial_{\bar{v}}X-\frac{\kappa_{sl}}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\partial(Y+\Phi[u])\,\partial_{\bar{v}}(Y+\Phi[u])-\frac{\kappa_{sl}}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\partial_{v}X\,\partial_{\bar{v}}X

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa_{sl}}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\partial(Y+\Phi[u])\,\partial_{\bar{v}}(Y+\Phi[u])\ ,

(4.18)

where we used that

\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\Bigl(-\partial_{\bar{v}}X\,\partial_{v}(\Phi[u]+Y)+\partial_{v}X\,\partial_{\bar{v}}(\Phi[u]+Y)\Bigr)=0

(4.19)

vanishes by integration by parts. Assembling eqs. (
4.13
), (
4.16
)-(
4.18
) we obtain the bosonic contribution to the partition function,

Z^{B}_{sl}(\tau,z_{B})Z^{B}_{y}(\tau,u)=\frac{e^{-\pi\frac{(z_{B}-\bar{z}_{B})^{2}}{2\tau_{2}}}}{\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\,|\theta_{1}(\tau,z_{B})|^{2}}\,{\rm exp}\Bigl(-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\big|\partial_{v}(y+\sqrt{\kappa}_{sl}\Phi[u])\big|^{2}\Bigr)\,\times\\
\times\,{\rm exp}\Bigl(-\frac{\kappa_{sl}+2}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\big|\partial_{v}(Y+\Phi[z_{B}])\big|^{2}+\frac{\kappa_{sl}}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\big|\partial_{v}(Y+\Phi[u])\big|^{2}\Bigr)\ .

(4.20)

4.2
The coset partition function

The fermion action reads

S_{sl}^{F}=\int\frac{\text{d}^{2}v}{2\pi}\left[\psi_{sl}^{+}(\partial_{\bar{v}}+A_{\bar{v}})\psi^{-}_{sl}+\psi^{-}_{sl}(\partial_{\bar{v}}-A_{\bar{v}})\psi^{+}_{sl}+\tilde{\psi}^{+}_{sl}(\partial_{v}-A_{v})\tilde{\psi}^{-}_{sl}+\tilde{\psi}^{-}_{sl}(\partial_{v}+A_{v})\tilde{\psi}^{+}_{sl}\right]\ .

(4.21)

The computation of the fermion path integral again reduces to the space transverse to the gauge action

Z^{F}_{sl}(\tau,z_{F})=\int\mathcal{D}\psi^{+}_{sl}\,\mathcal{D}\psi^{-}_{sl}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\psi}^{+}_{sl}\,\mathcal{D}\tilde{\psi}^{-}_{sl}\,e^{-S_{sl}^{F}}\ .

(4.22)

One proceeds as in Section
3.2
and we have
Eguchi:2004yi
;
Eguchi:2010cb

Z^{F}_{sl}(\tau,z_{F})=e^{\frac{\pi}{2\tau_{2}}(z_{F}-\bar{z}_{F})^{2}}\frac{|\theta_{1}(\tau,z_{F})|^{2}}{|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}\,{\rm exp}\left(\frac{2}{\pi}\int\text{d}^{2}v\,\left|\partial_{v}(Y+\Phi[z_{F}])\right|^{2}\right)\ .

(4.23)

All together, from eqs. (
4.8
), (
4.20
), and (
4.23
) we obtain

Z^{\frac{\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathds{R})}{\mathfrak{u}(1)}}(\tau,z)=\,\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\,|\eta(\tau)|^{2}\,e^{-\frac{2\pi}{\tau_{2}}\frac{\kappa_{sl}+4}{\kappa_{sl}}z_{2}^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{1}\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{2}\,\frac{|\theta_{1}(\tau,u+\frac{\kappa_{sl}+2}{\kappa_{sl}}z)|^{2}}{|\theta_{1}(\tau,u+\frac{2}{\kappa_{sl}}z)|^{2}}\,e^{-4\pi\frac{u_{2}z_{2}}{\tau_{2}}}\\
\times{\rm exp}\Bigl(\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits\kern-0.99998pt\text{d}^{2}v\Bigl[-\tfrac{\kappa_{sl}+2}{\pi}\left|\partial_{v}(Y+\Phi[u+\tfrac{2}{\kappa_{sl}}z])\right|^{2}+\tfrac{\kappa_{sl}}{\pi}\left|\partial_{v}(Y+\Phi[u])\right|^{2}+\tfrac{2}{\pi}\left|\partial_{v}(Y+\Phi[u+\tfrac{\kappa_{sl}+2}{\kappa_{sl}}z])\right|^{2}\Bigr]\Bigr)\\
\times\int\mathcal{D}y\,{\rm exp}\Bigl(-\tfrac{1}{\pi}\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits\kern-0.99998pt\text{d}^{2}v\,|\partial_{v}(y+\sqrt{\kappa}_{sl}\Phi[u])|^{2}\Bigr)\ .

(4.24)

Notice that both
y
and
\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}\,\Phi[u]
have radius
\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}
, so that
y+\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}\,\Phi[u]
obeys the periodicity conditions

\displaystyle(y+\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}\,\Phi[u])(v+2\pi,\bar{v}+2\pi)

\displaystyle=(y+\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}\,\Phi[u])(v,\bar{v})-2\pi\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}(m_{1}+s_{1})\ ,

(4.25)

\displaystyle(y+\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}\,\Phi[u])(v+2\pi\tau,\bar{v}+2\pi\bar{\tau})

\displaystyle=(y+\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}\,\Phi[u])(v,\bar{v})+2\pi\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}(m_{2}+s_{2})\ .

(4.26)

We can then identify the last line of eq. (
4.24
) as the partition function of a twisted compact boson of radius
\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}
,
i.e.

\int\mathcal{D}y\,{\rm exp}\Bigl(-\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits\kern-0.99998pt\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\text d^2v}{\pi}$}}\,|\partial_{v}(y+\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}\,\Phi[u])|^{2}\Bigr)=\frac{\sqrt{\kappa_{sl}}}{\sqrt{\tau_{2}}|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathds{Z}}$}}{\rm exp}\Bigl(-\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.4pt}{}{\pi\kappa_{sl} }{\tau_2}$}}|m_{1}\tau+m_{2}+u|^{2}\Bigr)\ .

(4.27)

Since
\partial_{v}\Phi[u]
is linear in
u
, the second line in (
4.24
) simplifies,

{\rm exp}\biggl[\biggl(-\frac{4(\kappa_{sl}+2)}{\pi\kappa_{sl}}+\frac{2(\kappa_{sl}+2)^{2}}{\pi\kappa_{sl}^{2}}\biggr)|\partial_{v}\Phi[z]|^{2}\mathop{\mathord{\raise 0.49991pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}\nolimits\kern-0.99998pt\text{d}^{2}v\biggr]={\rm exp}\left(\frac{2\pi}{\tau_{2}}\frac{\kappa_{sl}+2}{\kappa_{sl}}|z|^{2}\right)\ ,

(4.28)

and eq. (
4.24
) becomes
13
13

13

Our computation is not sensitive to the overall normalization. However, as was done in
Eguchi:2010cb
, this can in principle be fixed by studying the decomposition into characters.

Z^{\frac{\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathds{R})}{\mathfrak{u}(1)}}(\tau,z)=e^{\frac{2\pi}{\tau_{2}}(\frac{\kappa_{sl}+2}{\kappa_{sl}}|z|^{2}-\frac{\kappa_{sl}+4}{\kappa_{sl}}z_{2}^{2})}\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{1}\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{2}\,\frac{|\theta_{1}(\tau,u+\frac{\kappa_{sl}+2}{\kappa_{sl}}z)|^{2}}{|\theta_{1}(\tau,u+\frac{2}{\kappa_{sl}}z)|^{2}}\\
\times\,e^{-\frac{4\pi u_{2}z_{2}}{\tau_{2}}}\sum_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathds{Z}}e^{-\frac{\pi\kappa_{sl}}{\tau_{2}}|m_{1}\tau+m_{2}+u|^{2}}\ ,

(4.29)

which agrees with the axial gauge partition function computed in
Eguchi:2010cb
.

4.3
Evaluating the gauge field zero mode integrals

Let us review how the partition function (
4.29
) can be decomposed into characters. This subsection is essentially a summary of
Maldacena:2000kv
;
Hanany:2002ev
;
Eguchi:2010cb
and has the purpose to warm up in view of the decomposition into characters of the supertube partition function we discuss in Section
6
.

We begin by combining the integrals over
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
with the sum over
m_{1},m_{2}
in (
4.29
) into an integral over the complex plane. Using eq. (
A.8
) we find

Z^{\frac{\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathds{R})}{\mathfrak{u}(1)}}(\tau,z)=e^{\frac{2\pi}{\tau_{2}}(\frac{\kappa_{sl}+2}{\kappa_{sl}}|z|^{2}-\frac{\kappa_{sl}+4}{\kappa_{sl}}z_{2}^{2})}\int_{\mathds{C}}\frac{\text{d}^{2}u}{\tau_{2}}\,\frac{|\theta_{1}(\tau,u+\frac{\kappa_{sl}+2}{\kappa_{sl}}z)|^{2}}{|\theta_{1}(\tau,u+\frac{2}{\kappa_{sl}}z)|^{2}}\,e^{-\frac{4\pi u_{2}z_{2}}{\tau_{2}}}e^{-\frac{\pi\kappa_{sl}}{\tau_{2}}|u|^{2}}\ ,

(4.30)

where
\frac{\text{d}^{2}u}{\tau_{2}}=\text{d}s_{1}\text{d}s_{2}
. This allows us to shift the integration variable according to
u\mapsto u-\frac{2}{\kappa_{sl}}z
,

\displaystyle Z^{\frac{\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathds{R})}{\mathfrak{u}(1)}}(\tau,z)

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{2\pi}{\tau_{2}}(\frac{\kappa_{sl}+2}{\kappa_{sl}}|z|^{2}-z_{2}^{2})}\int_{\mathds{C}}\frac{\text{d}^{2}u}{\tau_{2}}\,\frac{|\theta_{1}(\tau,u+z)|^{2}}{|\theta_{1}(\tau,u)|^{2}}\,e^{-\frac{4\pi u_{2}z_{2}}{\tau_{2}}}e^{-\frac{\pi\kappa_{sl}}{\tau_{2}}|u-\frac{2}{\kappa_{sl}}z|^{2}}

(4.31)

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{2\pi}{\tau_{2}}(\frac{\kappa_{sl}+2}{\kappa_{sl}}|z|^{2}-z_{2}^{2})}\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{1}\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}s_{2}\,\frac{|\theta_{1}(\tau,s_{1}\tau+s_{2}+z)|^{2}}{|\theta_{1}(\tau,s_{1}\tau+s_{2})|^{2}}e^{-4\pi s_{1}z_{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt\times\,\sum_{w,m\in\mathds{Z}}e^{-\frac{\pi\kappa_{sl}}{\tau_{2}}|(s_{1}+w)\tau+(s_{2}+m)-\frac{2}{\kappa_{sl}}z|^{2}}\ .

(4.32)

Integral over
\bm{s_{2}}
:

Let us first perform the integral over
s_{2}
. In order to do so, we expand the Theta functions in the numerator, see eq. (
A.2a
)

|\theta_{1}(\tau,u+z)|^{2}=\sum_{n_{f}^{sl},\bar{n}_{f}^{sl}\in\mathds{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}(n_{f}^{sl})^{2}}\bar{q}^{\frac{1}{2}(\bar{n}_{f}^{sl})^{2}}\,e^{2\pi is_{1}\tau_{1}(n_{f}^{sl}-\bar{n}_{f}^{sl})}\,e^{-2\pi s_{1}\tau_{2}(n_{f}^{sl}+\bar{n}_{f}^{sl})}\\[-11.38092pt]
\times\,e^{2\pi i(zn_{f}^{sl}-\bar{z}\bar{n}_{f}^{sl})}\,{\rm exp}\left(2\pi is_{2}(n_{f}^{sl}-\bar{n}_{f}^{sl})\right)\ ,

(4.33)

and use the identity
Pakman:2003kh
;
Israel:2004ir
